28 BRIGHT 

SPARKS 

It is many an Amiga user's 
dream to make a living from 
the machine we all love, but 
few actually achieve it. This 
month we profile some of 
those success stories. 

Come behind the scenes 
with CU AMIGA as we chart 
the rise of these Amiga mae- 
stros. Read how these 
people have used the Amiga 
to touch many aspects of our 
lives. From special effects on 
top TV shows to training 
London Underground drivers, 
the Amiga's influence has 
spread far and wide 

Find out what you need to 
turn your favourite machine 
into a money spinner. Do you 
have what it takes to be a 
success? 



1 47 AMIGA WORKSHOP 

If you're interested in the more serious side- of the Amiga, 
then this is the place lor you. Each month odr team of

ICD VIPER TO 
BITE IN JUNE 

Following in the footsteps of virtually every 
other hardware manufacturer, ICD have 
announced an upcoming expansion for Ihe 
Amiga 1200. 

The Viper 1230 is a number of expansions in 
one (hough The basic configuration of the 
board wiit be an '030 running at 40MHr with an 
optional 68882 maths coprocessor and a real 
time battery backed clock. 
Extra Ram will be available through a SIMM 
slot on thg board which will accept standard 32- 
bit modules of up to 64Mb - more than enough 
for 'most applications. 

Perhaps the most important feature is the fur- 
ther expansion capability offered by the Viper 
DMA port. Tints is cunningly situated so that fur- 
ther expansion devices can be plugged in from 
the rear 'expansion' slot on the A1200 and con- 
nect into the main Vjper board in the trapdoor 
One of ihe first expansions to be released will 
be a SCSI-2 controller, wfiich will ailow very 
high speed (up to 1 0Mb/sacond) data commu- 
nication with SCSI and SCSI-2 devices. There 
will be an internal socket to allow an internal 
2.5 men SCSI drive lo be used, though this 
could be a significant drain on power. Other 
interlaces are planned for fhe future. 
ICD plans to distribute the Viper in the UK 
through Power Computing who are on 0234

OUT TO LUNCH 

There have been some unusual themes for games but Mind scape's up coming Pierre Le Chef lakes the 
biscuit - literally. Pierre is a world renowned master chef capable of cooking up some incredible dishes. 
The only problem he has is that the ingredients often don't want to end up as a main course. II that wasn't 
enough he has an arch rival, Le Chef Noir. who is intent on ruining Pierre's reputation. 

The aim of this game then is to guide Pierre around 40 challenging platform -based levels in an attempt 
to collect ihB ingredients he needs for his famous creations Ai the same time he's got to fend off the 
unwanted attentions of various bugs and diseases, which like nothing better than a bit of fresh food, and 

keep Le Chef rJoir away from his 
store room. 

Set across 6 countries, each 
with its own distinctive theme 
revolving around their national 
dishes and music, this platformer 
is sure to please fans oi the cute 
character genre. There'll be 
secret levels, unique power-ups 
and over 1000 frames of humor- 
ous character animations to keep 
you entertained. 

Out to Lunch is scheduled for 
an October release, Contacl 
Mmdscape on 0444 245333 for 
more information.

» Quito a few multimedia systems are 
available for the Amiga but by lar the 
easiest (and indeed one of the most

PAGE PLAY! 

The basic concept behind 
HyperBook is very similar to that 
other great wlomnation system, ihe 
good old book (remember them*?). 
Like a real book, all the information 
that you wish to present is held within 
"pages' which in turn are linked 
together to form a book (or a 'stack', 
as the multimedia purists prefer). 
Each page within your book can con- 
lain any combination of lour basic 
'objects' - buttons, noies. drawings 
and lists These objects can then be 
assigned one of eight actions which 
are performed when the user clicks 
on Ihem with Ihe left mouse button. 
Well be covering them in detail later 
on, but basically you can make any 



object display a picture, show a tex| 
file, run an external program or - pro- 
viding you've got ARexx - you can 
even control the actions of olher 

peripherals and software through 
AReo 'ports' <!his sort of thing is for 
Ihe teenies only, though). 

Like a real book, your HyperBook 
applications will be rather limited if 
Ihey contain only one page, so sev- 
eral pages need to be defined in 
order to allow a far greater amount of 
information 1o be held. In turn r these 
pages need to be linked by creating 
objects on each page that display 
other pages once they've been 
clicked upon. Anyway, enough of the 
theory- gel stock in. Turn on your 
Amiga, insert our HyperBook 
coverdisk in the internal drive and 
once Workbench has loaded, double 
click on the HyperBook icon to enter 
ihe fascinating world of HyperBook 
multimedia, .

ASSIGNING ACTIONS TO OBJECTS 



In order tor your HyperBook stacks 
1o do anything even remotely useful, 
you must create objects that, in turn, 
must have actions assigned to them, 
When you first create an object 
though, HyperBook doesn't automat- 
ical ly assign an action to it. This you 
must do yourself. HyperBook allows 
you to assign any one ol eight differ- 
ent actions lo an object which allow 
the object to perform a particular 
task when the user clicks on it with 
the right mouse button. 
To assign an action to an object,

each. Each ofthese gadgets can be 
selected lo assign an aciign to an 
object. Here's a quick rundown of 
what each does. 

NO ACTION 

Nol Surprisingly. Ihe Mo Action 
option causes the gadget to do 
absolutely nothing when the user 
Clicks on it. This is the very exciting 

default setting. 

SHOW PICTURE 

Another pretty obvious option this. 
Show Picture allows you to attach an 
IFF picture to an object so lhal when 
the user clicks on thai object the 
picture is displayed. Note that 
although HyperBook normally only 
runs in medium or high resolution, 
Ihe picture wilt be displayed on a 
separate screen using its native 
screen mode I" HyperBook can't han- 
dle AGA pics though!).

conventional experience. The 
Amiga was created by people 
this, so it's not really surprising that 
a lot of very 'creative' types use it. 
Along the way some of them may 
achieve fame and notoriety - some 
may even make a living - but from 
the graphics artists to the multime- 
dia maestros they all have one thing 
in Common - a belief in the Amiga 
not just as a computer, more as a

TOBIAS RICHTER 

GRAPHIC ARTIST 

SHU only 25 years of age. Tobias fiichter already 
has an enviable reputation as art Amiga graphic 
artist. His first public domain demos began to 
appear live years ago and have gradually 
in« eased In complexity with each new release 
Most ol his 3D animations and graphic disks 
adopt a sci 4 theme, with the USS Enterprise fig- 
uring largely in the scheme of" things, And no 
wonder, as Tobias musl be one of the biggest 
Star Trek nuts ever! 

That's not fa say the talented German artist 
has not explored other avenues, as he's also pro- 
duced a rich variety of animations covering 
everything from a speeding sports car to a flypast 
from an F 1 S Tighter. Other graphic disks have 
included anything from stoll-lrle portraits to reirrler- 
pretations of classic films. Recently, Tobias has 
also moved into video production. Using the pro- 




cessing power of five different Amigas he's chan- 
nelled his excellent animation skills and graphical 
prowess into producing some stunning-tooking 
videos, The best ot these is undoubtedly Space 
Wars, a five- mingle movie featuring all manner of 
SF-related hardware, including Imperial Cruisers. 



X Wing and Tie-fighters, the Enterpnse and a 

host ol other famous sci-fi icons, 

Another is a Trelr-based mini- movie Lasting 
eight-minutes involving the Enterprise engaging a 
Klmgon cruiser. A third. Mouse Track, was a 
demo reel made lor the German distributor of 
Reflections, an excellent 3D animation package 
that Tobias uses for the majority of his wo*, 

He's produced a handful of commercial anima- 
tions lor industry and TV and has worked on the 
German equivalent of Spitting image called Hurra 
DeutSCbland 

He is writing a book about Amiga animation, 
writes regularly tor the German Amiga mags and 
has provided the graphics for a wide range ol 
games including the likes of Oft Imperium, 
Cubulus, and Bundesiiga Manager Professional. 
His latest project is his most promising - he's 
been signed up to produce some computer ani- 
mations for Moontrap 2, a new sci-h movie due 
for release in i &94, He'll be producing ali the 
computer displays and on-screen graphics.

TEAM 17 

SOFTWARE HOUSE 

The recent recipients ol a coveted 
Golden Joystick for Software House 
of the Year, Team 17 continue to go 
from strength to strength. Founded 
in December 1 990, they've quickly 
built up an enviable track record of 
hit after number one hit. And best of 
all, they've done so by specialising in 
Amiga games. PC owners and con- 
sole kids have had to look on wilh 
envy, as Amiga owners have 
enjoyed some of the best computer 
games produced over the last couple 
ol years 

Ol course, you don't get any- 



where without a lot of hard work, and 

its two bunding directors, Martyn 
Brown and Mick Robinson, have cer- 
tainly put a lol of graft into the 
company since its launch. Martyn 
Brown, who heads up the design 
and development side of Team 17, 
was originally head honcho at PD 
library. 17- B«t Software, where his 
contacts with Amiga programmers, 
musicians and alists convinced him 
he could produce better games than 
many of the efforts being released 
onto the market at thn time. Mick 
Robinson, meanwhile, had been 
steadily building up the Microbyte 
chain of game stores with flagship 
premises in Sheffield's Meadowhall 
and Oxford Street in London. 




It was Mick's financial expertise 
and Martyn s creative management 
that finally convinced the pair to join 
forces and establish a new software 
house. 

Although their slated intention 
was to release 1 Mb-oniy titles, the 
first Team 17 release was actually a 



51 2K game. Futl Contact 
was a fast- paced karate- 
inspired beat "em up and 
was released at a budget 
busting price of just £9.99, 
Not surprisingly, it immedi- 
ately shot to the top of Ihe 
budget charts and estab- 
lished Team 17 as a 
top-quality software house. 
Their first lull price game. 
Alien Breed, also did exceptionally 
well, lopping the charts yet again, 
and the rest, as Ihey say, is history. 
With another live full-price games 
lined up Over the course of the next 
twelve months . plus another eight 
budget releases, 1993 promises to 
be their busiest year yet! 



ERIC SCHWARTZ BABYLON 5/NEWTEK 



SCIENCE FICTION SHOW/DESKTOP VIDEO 




Mention Amiga animation and there's a good chance 
that you'll think of Eric Schwartz. Over the past live 
years, his zany cartoons, featuring the likes ol Flip the 
Frog and Amy the Squirrel, have consistently lopped 
the PD charts arid been raved about in the computer 
press. Using Gold Disk's MovieSelter, various paint 
packages, and an abundance of creative talent, Eric 
has a back catalogue of more than 40 animation 
disks, each one a 
classic example of 
its genre. From 
Space Shuttles 
playing shuttle- 
cock inouler 
space to an invisi- 
ble Stealth 
Bomber sneaking 
up on another plane and tapping its wings, Eric has a 
knack for absorbing the comic elements out of a situa- 
tion and inflating them to gigantic proportions. 

Still attending art college, and only just turned 2Q, 
ihe American -based artist has already dipped a cau- 
tious toe into the commercial world. Team 17's 
recently-released Svperfrog game featured a three- 
minute demo from Eric and he's lined up several more 
projects m the near future. There's also ihe possibility 
of a series of short animated movies in the not-toc 
distant future. Eric has even attracted the interest of 
the Disney organisation, which uses Amigas exten- 
sively at ils Dublin-based animation college, and is all 
set to break into the world ol commercial animation 
once he's completed his studies. 




Touted as one of the hottest sci-fr shows of 
recent times, Babyfon 5 received its US pre- 
miere towards the end ol February. The 
two-hour long pilot movie, set on-board a huge 
sprawling space station, was an instant hit and 
filming has already started on 26 one-hour 
episodes to be screened later this year. What 
makes the Show stand out from the current glul 
of sci-fi shows, however, is that most of the 
pilot's special effects were created using an 
Amiga and NewTek's Video Toaster hardware. 

Rejecting traditional model-based anima- 
tions, the show's producers decided to opt lor 




an A2QQ0 and up lo eight Video Toasters as a 
more economical FX package. The results, as 
anyone who has seen the show will testify, are 
little short ct amazing. Commenting in a recent 
issue of sci-fi fantasy magazine. Startiurst, 
director Richard Complon went on record to 
say: 'I just couldn't conceive of the idea that a 
computer, without photographing any models 
at all, coy Id generate the kind ol graphics that 
would lum out to be as realisljc as they are. 

NewTek's all-in-one broadcast quality work- 
stalion was launched in October 1990 and was 
en instant success, being hailed as the 'com- 
plete video studio in a box'. Paul 
Montgomery and Tim Jemson. Ihe 
development team behind Ihe Toaster, 
set out to create a DTV environment 
that was 'good enough lor the film 
indusiry. out also cheap enough lor the 
home user to enjoy'. 

Today, the Toaster has been used 
extensively in commercial's, TV shows 
and movies, including Star Trek Vt. 
Both Montgomery and Jenison are 
fanatical Star Trsk fans, so when 
Paramount approached them to work 
on one of Ihe Trek movies they jumped 
al the chance, Interestingly, the team 
also claim to have held back the mar- 
keting ot the Video Toaster until they 
could achieve a satisfactory beam 
down effect! 



AEON DESIGN 

TRAIN SIMULATOR 

Before its recent returtushmenf (he London 
Transport Museum used lo have a couple of 
Amigas On display. They weren't being shown off 
as pieces of outdated technology though. Two 
A2Q0Q5 were simulating some of the trains of 
yesteryear (hat used to roam up and down the 
subterranean ihouroughfares. carrying passen- 
gers to and Irom their places of work. 

The software and hardware were put together 
by a team called Aeon Design, who also pro- 
duced an animation which simulated the progress 
o< the train up and down the line. In the 1 890 sim- 
ulator, the throttle control was a giant rheostat or 
variable resistor. This was connected up through 
an interface to the Amiga which then read the 




position ol the lever and adjusted the animation 
accordingly. 

, The 1 990 simulator had a more up to date 
throttle lever (which was connected m exactly the 
same way) but the Amiga also controlled digital 
displays showing the train's actual speed and its 
optimum speed, as they are displayed in the 
more modem trains, 

The Amiga also played back samples when 
the train was in ihe station, taken from actual 
journeys on the Underground 

The train simulator was quite a hit at the 
museum among the various dignitaries and nota- 
bles who visited it, including former transport 
secretary Cecil Parkinson. Members ol the gen- 
eral public were nol so impressed, complaining 
that the simulator was unrealistic - alter all it 
never broke down, it was very clean and it was 
always on time.

REAL TIME GRAPHICS 

TV SHOW GRAPHICS 

Would it sutpnse you lo learn that 
some of the most impressive com- 
puter graphics on TV at (tie moment 
are produced by a company using an 
Amiga? Well, actually Itiey use sev- 
aral but the principle still holds good! 
That company is Real Time Graphics 
and (hey work on many top TV 
snows, including Gafnesfnaster and 
Sky TV's Games World. 

Real Time is headed up by Iwo 
guys with many years experience In Doth television and Amiga graphics. 
Simon Dunslan and Christopher Mills started Iheir TV careers in 1 965 as 
freelance designers on Channel 4 s Network 7. a Jane! Street-Porter 'yoof 
programme. Even way back then Simon was using an Amiga lo handle 
some of the graphics work. His first contacl with the machine came about as 
a result ol some game work he was doing tor a company called Ram Jam. 
Unfortunately they loided before the game was finished, but they dnJ leave 
him with one of the original 1000s. 

After the success^ Network 7 went ofl the air, Simon and Chris moved 
on lo a programme called' Sfar Test The concept for this show was that 
each week a celebnly would be interviewed by a pseudo computer and !he 
stars would be seen through one of Ihe computer's many cameras. The 
scope for computer graphics was 
obvious and Simon and Chris were 
involved in designing Ihe screen lay- 
out. The show was successful and a 
second series was commissioned. At 
this point the guys decided to set up 
a company to handle the work and 
Real Time was born. Business grew 
with more work coming in from othnr 
arty shows, including Star Chamber. 
a series of Caichpfrrase (?!!), 
Gamesmastsr and, now, Sky TV'S 
Games World 

Real Time's involvement in the shows extends all the way Irom Ihe title 
sequences to Games/Raster's famous Consoietation Zone' and it's all done 
with Amigas! For example, the stufl for Gamesmasler was done on A2000s 
running at SQMHiz with GVP boards installed. It would be a mistake, though, 
lo think that the guys are getting money 1or old rope as their Amiga anima- 
tions are highly developed pieces of work. 

The Consoietation Zone itself is an astonishingly complex piece of 
graphic work involving multiple video and Amiga layering. The sky is a Sim- 
ple drawing that's been felted and faded by a Digital Video Effects unit and 
an animated sea is overlaid on it. The sea is in turn overlaid by another ani- 
mation, usually Patrick Moore s head, which has been tilled and stretched 
by a OVE. They then use two Amigas to create animated maps of Patrick's 
lace which are run slightly out of phase to create a lime-lapse effect. Wow! 
All (hat elfort for a sequence running no more than a couple of minutes. It's 
sad then thai for a show that uses Amigas so much they spend all their time 
talking about consoles! 

Simon is particularly excited about their latest work on Games World as. 
ft has allowed them to experimant with lip syncing an animated character. 
One ol the episodes features a female character who hands out games 
advice and cheats, rather like Patrick Moore on Gamesmaster. The differ- 
ence here inough is that the character is completely animated with a real-life 
actress providing the voice. The challenge then was to make the character 
seem as realistic as possible whilst still retaining the computer leel. This 

they have achieved very suc- 
cessfully. Whilst not totally 
happy with Ihe sync they plan to 
arid many more frames to the 
lips lo get them as dose to per- 
lect as possible. 

Real Time have got a lot of 
projects in the pipeline but 
they're still very much hush- 
hush. We'll keep you up to dale 
with what they do. You don't 
want lo rri 55 the chance lo 
catch your favourite machine 
being stretched to the limit. 





NIK WILLIAMS 

ncniREWARE 

Nik Williams originally hit the 
Amiga press with Ihe launch of 
Pjctureware. a set of licensed 
clip-art images which 
addressed a market need. 
With a high quality video cam- 
era and a variety of Amiga 
equipment, he travelled the 
highways and byways collect- 
ing an impressive library of 
images. Nik then sold Ihe sets 
as licenseware. charging 
insignrlicani amounts for disks full of 
top quality images. 

He used his knowledge of the 
Amiga, and the applications soft- 
ware available on it. to create stand 
alone multimedia terminals. The 
first was used as an interactive 
show guide last year. Galled a 
Visual Presentation Centre, it is 
really a super tower system which 
carl accept any current Amiga com- 
puter, including the CDTV. Once an 
Amiga has been installed il is then 
connected to the inpul device, a 16 
button touch sensitive keyboard. 
This has been constructed in such a 
way lhat a user-delmed template 
can be slipped in on top. 

For the units shown so far Nik 
has relied on a number of software 




packages, but Scale is used to drive 

the system in use. The latest devel- 
opment is the implementation of 
'through Ihe glass' technology, 
which allows the unit to be used by 
people physically separated from it. 
as in a shop-window type situation. 
This means that the units could be 
used by passers-by even if the 
shop/showroom wasn't even open. 
an idea which is currently being 
researched by Ford. 

His hope is that such systems 
will be used in various situations 
where people may need information 
but which are not permanently 
staffed: railway stations, show- 
rooms, estate agencies, hotels and 
shopping complexes are all pnme 
candidates,. 






PORTADOWN 
PLANETARIUM 

MULTIMEDIA INFORMATION SYSTEM 

The Stars are a long way away. The light coming from mosl of them takes gen- 
erations to reach us. Many of these celestial bodies may no longer exist, but 
could have been blown apart years ago. 

The light from distant stars *s of direct interest to the Armagh Planetarium, 
and the planetarium is of direct interest to us Why? Because out of the many 
exhibits and interactive learning tools employed there, some ol the most popu- 
lar are Amigas. They are cheap, versatile and easy to use. The planetarium 
soon realised .the benefits of an exhibit which couW be changed simply by 
loading up some new software. Instead of expensive to produce, difficult to 
maintain and short-lived physical exhibits, the Amiga otters an interactive plat- 
form which can be changed lo suit current needs. 

The Amigas, in conjunction with A590 hard disks, are used to drive 
laserdisk devices containing stills and video sequences, The Amiga simultane- 
ously displays background informalion on another screen, explaining various 
aspects about... well, whatever the pictures are about. 

There are three Amigas in use. displaying information on the stars and how 
they are formed. Mars, and the weather. Unfortunately many visitors have 
recognised the controlling Amiga, and since the software takes some time lc 
set up precautions in the form of 
large keyboard protectors are nec- 
essary to prevent errant visitors 
doing a soft resel. 

Because these sylems can be 
re-used they have a virtually infinite 
iifeiime - they will only become 
redundant when a more revolution- 
ary form ol multimedia information 
station is available, perhaps one 
based around virtual reality. II so, 
the only commercially proven VR 
system, once again, is based on the 
Amiga platform.

JEFF WALKER 

MAGAZINE PUBLISHER 

Work started on setting up Just Amtga Monthly 
{JAM] in August 1 990, sue months after Jeff 
Walker had resigned from his previous job as 
Editor of Amiga Computing. He wanted to produce 
a 'serious Amiga enthusiast magazine and lo use 
the Amiga to do it . 

In September of that year the first issue was 
published, reproduced from original artwork 
printed on a Canon BJ-1 30 bubble jet. Fewer than 
1 00 copies were sold. But then it WAS mail-order 
only and it would take- a while for the great 
unwashed (that's you lot) to cotton-on to the 
adverts. 

After setting-up costs and another print run for 



the second issue, the capital was 

dwindling fast. 

Throwing caution to the wind 
(heck, it's on'y money') Jeff gambled 
the best part ol what was left in a 
small stand at Ihe 1 990 Commodore 
Show. From than on he's never 
looked back. 

In Ihose days JAM was being pro- 
duced with Professional Page t.3 on 
a standard Amiga 2000 with 3Mb of 
RAM and a 20Mb hard drive. With Ihe 
first real "positive cashflow' Jeff 
bought a second -hand A2630 accelerator card! 
which boosted his RAM by 4Mb and gave him 
greater speed in one tell swoop. 

Today, he has an A500, 2000 and 3000. Two 
massive hard drives, still barely 30 per cent full. 




store all the JAM data and leave 
plenty of room for the future. The 

acquisition of an Epson GT-&000 
flatbed scanner has helped to 
speed-up production and increase 
the quality of the mag. 

Jeff would like to see JAM more 
widely read. Putting n on the news- 

S stand would probably be a mi slake 
as there are already so many Amiga 
mags to choose from. But the British 
are not 'joiners', on the whole they 
pre'er not to commit themselves 
and have an inexplicable suspicion about sub- 
scribing to anyttiing, so although J^Mhas 
thousands of readers worldwide, the big battle at 
home has yet to be won. If you'd like to know 
more you can contact JAM on: 0B95 274440. 



ALTERNATIVE IMAGE 

GRAPHICS COMPANY 

There are some people who have been 
around the Amiga scene for yeans, and 
then there are the people that remember 
the first lot ol people when they were new- 
comers, One such group of people run 
Attemafive Image. 

The expertise combined in Yuri Large 
and Henri Bujko is the force behind the 
company. They produce corporate graph- 
ics for presentations, including slides and 
animations, Clients include Bass. Walkers 
crisps and Caterpillar, the industrial plant equipment manufacturer. 

Integrated into the heart of then hi- band and SP U-matiC video equipment 
are a host Of Amiga 2000s. The boxes may be old. but they are Sturdy and 




house a number of surprises which would put an A4000 to shame. Every 
piece of notable video hardware has been in and out of these machines at 
some time or another, because Alternative image also operate as 
Amiga dealers. 

The video side ot their work used to /evolve around rock bands, but 
unfortunately most of the bands they produced vids lor soon split up or 
'fizzled out - videos aren't everything. Now they are producing filness 
videos which are sold through retail chains like W.H. Smith. 

However this may change. They may be lured back into the music 
side of the business if their current ventures with German company 
Studk) 7 pay off. Studio 7 were behind the 3- Lux 2 vid reviewed in 
these hallowed pages back in March and are now working on a title 
called X-Mix Volume 1 in conjunction with Allernalive Image. Because 
these projects work on a royalty basis there well be no money coming in 
from them until some time after they are completed. This means that

video work at night. It may sound I ke tiard work, and it certainly is, but it is 
vaned and interesting we were assured by Henri. 



IRA CURTIS COLEMAN GJ PRODUCTIONS 



COMPUTER FX 

Ira Curtis Coleman is a design engi- 
neer with a neat sideline in computer 
hardware special effects. 

One of ihe mam aims of any film 
or TV show is to make the action as 
realistic as possible Whether it's set 
on an alien planet or in some big city 
dealer's office, youVe got to believe 
that the actors are really I here. 
Equally, no matter what the location 
it's usual lor the plot to reflect real 
life in some way. These days that 
usually involves sticking a computer 
in every other shot. It's very difficult 
to shoot a scene which involves 
computers because every screen 
flickers lo a greater or lesser extent 
and. if that flicker is being viewed via 
a machine which also flickers, i,e. 
the camera's shutter, it can cause 
Ihe computer display to appear 




Wank. The normal way lo avoid this 
is to lock the camera's shutter to the 

speed of the computer screen. This 
can cause a problem in long shots 
because the sound can gradually 
drift Out of sync with the vision. 

This is where Ira comes in - not 
only does he design the computer 
screen displays you see he also has 
invented numerous bits of hardware 
that keeplhese screens in sync with 
the cameras, His engineering back- 
ground has enabled him to 
understand how the camera and 
lighting works. 

His list of credits stretches over 
1 years and reads like a who's who 
of TV and films with titles like Alien, 
Outiander. Brazil, A View to a Kill, 
Superman. Spies Like Us and, more 
recently. Splitting H0irs. Inspector 
Morse. Taggart and Peak Practice. 

It would be nice to claim that he 
uses nothing but Amigas but the 
truth is that he uses a range Of 
machines. The type of machine used 
depends on what he's trying to 
achieve. For instance, on ITV's one- 
ofl drama. Seekers, he used an 
Amiga in a scene where one charac- 
ter was being fingerpnnted. Despite 
the fact that it was disguised as a PC 
he used it to draw and display the 
lingerprint on a screen.

W. INDUSTRIES 

VIRTUAL REALITY ON THE AMIGA 

During 1990, you just couldn'i escape 
the hype sifrrounding Virtual Reality 
{VR). Three years laier and you could 
be forgiven for wondering what all the 
hype was about. 

For those unaware, Vft is a com- 
puter technology that immerses 
individuals in a 3D computer gener- 
ated world where powerful soltware 
allows the player lo piay against each 
other or a computer-controlled oppo- 
nent. The prime exponent of the 
technology in this country is W. 
Industries Since launching 
VirtuaNtyTM lOOOStJ- the wood's first VH entertainment system - in March 
1991 , the company das invented a series of intriguing aracade games to 
take advantage ol the technology. 

Either seated withiri an enclosed 3D environment or wearing a special 
head mask, the player enters a virtual world where a I actions within the 
environment are controlled by the use of a specially adapted joy&lick. Held in 
either hand, the space joystick is tracked by the computer and can appear in 
the virtual world as anything from a hard to a high lech photon sword, 

So what has all this to do with the Amiga, then? Simple, you'll soon be 
able lo experience VR in the comfort of your own home* W. Industries are 
currency working on a VR-headset designed for use with Ihe Amiga and 
which should, lingers crossed, be ready sometime in the new year. Price 
and exact specification 1 ; have yel to be finalised but it looks as if the VR rev- 
olution is finally upon .us. 

- Apparently, customised VR chips in the head set will help extend the 
Amiga's already superb polygon-handling routines still further and open up a 
whole new dimension lor 3D games. So hold onto your hats, in a ligurative 
simulated -3D environment kind of way.

EVELYN GLENNIE 

CLASSICAL MUSICIAN 

Hidden away in the wilds of 
Cambridgeshire is the recording stu- 
dio of the world's foremosl classical 
percussionist. And in foe head of the 
studio are three Amigas. 

Evelyn Glenn ie is a remarkably 
talented musician, having graduated 
from the Royal Academy of Music 
with a combined degree in both 
piano and percussion. It is her love 
of percussion that has become world 
famous. She has a unique lalent for 
playing anything that needs lo be hit. 
ranging from kettle drums to snares. 
xylophones to woodblocks, she plays 
foem all, and to watch her play is to 
be transfixed until the performance is 
finished. 

Herf/ance. Greg Malcar.gie, m 
conjunction with Andy Bishop of Blue 
Ribbon Soundworks have built an 
entire recording studio around an 
'040 Amiga 4000 and Bars & Pipes 
Pro software. 

The Amiga >s linked both to the 
recording and mixing equipment, and 
lo the instruments and triggering 
devices via a Tnple Play Plus MIDI 
interface. This excellent interface 
{another Blue Ribbon innovation) 
allows (he computer to communicate 
via 46 channels, three times as 
many as usually permitted under 
standard MIDI protocols. 




This means that Evelyn will play a 
tune on a controller keyboard and 
her performance is automatical y 
recorded as a series of MIDI events 
by Bars& Ptpes{B&P). 

Because the controller keyboard 
is not necessarily capable of produc- 
ing sounds of ils Own. B& P outputs 
the MIDI information to a sound 
module or other instrument. 

Having played one part of a per- 
formance, Evelyn can then move 
over to the 13 pad DrumKAT, her 
MIDI compatible drum kri and play 
the drum section to accompany ihe 
performance which she just 
recorded Bars & Pipes will automati- 
cally add the MIDI information Irom 
her drum hit to the keyboard informa- 
tion already stored in memory. 

As Greg says, The beauty of the 
Bars & Pipes Pro is that it's a com- 
pletely professional package foal 
anyone can use ' 



ALUgTER BRIMBLE 

It's not all raves and hallucinogenics when it comes to Amiga music White 
the popular aspiration is to release a 1 2" dance record , others are taking a 
different route. Amiga- veteran Allister Bnmhle for example, has just 
released an 1 1 -track CD alburr 

Allisler has been on the Amiga scene for years. He first made a name for 
himself with a series ol PD music demos. His demos nad always gone 
forough 1 7 Bit Software, and when Martin Brown launched the otf-shoot 
games software house Team 1 7, Allister was firmly in the frame when they 
needed a musician. 

Since then, he's written the soundtracks to such gems as Alien Breed, 
Project X, Assassin and Supertrog. His game soundtracks proved to be so 
popular, that he decided to re-record a tew of his best ones along with some 
completely new material, which became his debul album Sounds Digital. 
To record the CD, Allister used an Amiga 500 running Music X, which 
sequenced a Kurzweil K2000, Korg 01R/W, Yamaha TG77. Akai S950. 
Alesis SR1 S and a Roland D1 10. Effects and EQ were added with an Alesis 
Quadravertj, Microverb III, Aphex Aural Exciter, and an ihanez GE3101 31 
band graphic equaliser. As you can imagine, the original game soundtracks 
jusl don't compare to the CD remakes. A second CD is in the pipeline. 

All star is currently working on the music and sound effects for Team 
17's Suffer Spn'nf-slyle Overdrive, and an A1200-specilic version of Alien 
Breed 3, along with Interplay's The Lost Vikings and the Star Trek 25th 
Anniversary game. Then there's the work for the Sega and Nintendo devel- 
opers. Any music for the consoles 
has to he enlered in assembler, 
rather than written with a music utility. 
In other words, all the nole pitches, 
lengths, and data on the sounds 
themselves have to be entered as a 
machine code program! To get your 
mits on the Sounds Digital CD, send 
a cheque or postal order for £10.99

SCALA UK 

MULTIMEDIA 

Scala Multimedia is a multimedia 
authoring package which can con- 
trol m real time and at speed a 
video and an audio source {pro- 
vided the Amiga running it is fitted 
with a multi-port serial card}, and 
mix them together with various tex- 
tual, graphic and audio tricks. Barry- 
Thurston. Managing Director of 
Scala UK, likens it lo a piece of 
glue 'It can take the sounds liom 
Bars & Pipes, the graphics Irom 
Real 3D, it can talk to and display 
on a graphics card and glue them 
all together into one seamless pre- 
sentation 

Nochannel, Scaia's big brother 
and predecessor, came into being 
in Norway in 1 987 A Norwegian 
friend of the designer, John 
Bohmer, asked him if he knew of a 
system that could provide him with 
a textfgraphics channel to give 
information on his cable TV com- 
pany. At that time no such thing 
existed, so John invented rt! 
Infochannel was designed to be a 
permanently running system that 
could be updated in background at 
speed. 

After some success wilh 
Infochannel and Scala in 
Scandinavia, John decided to look 
at exporting it to the UK. He visited 
Commodore in October 1 990 lo 
enquire about^istributors and met 
up with Dennis Phillips and Barry, 
who were then working for 
Commodore, At thai time there was 
no one in the UK who could have 
handled Scala properly so Dennis 
decided lo leave Commodore to set 
up Scala UK. Barry was to stay on 
until March 1992 when he decided 
that he loo should devoie himself lo 
Scala. 

Despiie this late start Barry is 
vocal in his support for his product. 
'What we want.with Scala is for il to 
produce something that holds the 
attention. That is Ihe single most 
important aspect of multimedia. 
Fortunately, in the Amiga, we have 
a piece of hardware that is capable 
of producing high quality graphics 
and. sound at a reasonable price. 
When you combine that with Scala 
you have a truly cost-effective multi- 
media solution.' Despite this 
enthusiasm for his product and the 



medium in which it works. Barry 
doesn't believe in trying to define 
multimedia as he believes that the 
definition rests entirely on what you 
want to do with H. The Closest that 
one can come to an all encompass- 
ing, il slightly tongue-in-cheek, 
definition is by using one of two for- 
mulas: PC2D - MM; or M3T2T2Y = 
MM. The former is for those people 
who believe that by sticking a CD 
ROM drive on a PC you can 
achieve a multimedia station and 
the latter is for more enlightened 
folk who know that the true defini- 
tion ol multimedia is "My multimedia 
technology tower is taller than 
yours" 

The big objection thai any 
Amiga-based business tool has to 
Circumvent is that it's not on a PC. 
Notwithstanding that and Sarry's 
sense of humour, Scala and 
Infochannel have proved to be a

SIMPLICITY ITSELF 

Looking at the screenshots, you've

pler than most. There are no toons to

trol panel- 

The first time I saw the

open/dose, examine, 
take and talk. 
Alongside them are 
any items you might be 
carrying, but thai s it

PUZZLING 

The backbone of any adventure 
Qame, though, is the puzzle element, 
i* me puzzles are too easy, it spoils 
tie game. If they are loo illogical, the 
game becomes frustrating. With so 
*ew manipulation icons, the bulk of 
the puzzles in Nippon Safes are 
based around picking objects up and 
using them in dilferent locations. 
Once an object is collected, you 
cant put it down until it's served Us 
logical function, so it's best to collect 
everything you can. 

As each character works through 
their story, Ihey will occasionally 
bump into the other two. This lets 
you connect the three games 
together. For instance, at one stage 
Donna has to learn to mate tea. She 
goes to a tea shop to ask for instruc- 
tions, and while outside notices a 
poster offering a huge cash prize In a 
wrestling tournament. She bumps 

Dino and persuades him to go in 
for it, If you play as Dine, you are 
walking through the market square 
when you bump into Donna and She 
convinces you to enter the tourna- 
ment. The fun part of playing all 
three is seeing each scene from the 
various viewpoints. 

The game is vary funny, righl 
from the start. Most of the humour 
comes from the conversations the 
Characters have with other people 
and themse-ves. Dino's naive 
insights into lite, the universe and 
everything are amazing, and the 
smart Alec answers you'll receive 
from Doug Nuts every time you try 
something stupid have to be seen! 

Nippon Sates isn't the mosl origi- 
nal graphic adventure, nor is it the 
most challenging. It is a playable and 
entertaining game however, and if 
<au're looking lor something to while 
away a week or two, yCu could Cer- 
tainty do a tot worse. &h

Cricket is a game Dial seems to be 
severely misun (e retried. Little do mast

much trying lo hit the ball, as defend tiinv 
sell. A blislefing ball rnjhi down the line 
leaves the kind at marl you'll be boasting 
about tor a long time, This kind dI lile-or- 
bruisK altitude can I be simulated on a 
computer, and is a paint whitli I els this 
gprne down. An -n Junes feature would 
have teen excellent. Alter all, who can 
large) Mi lie Catting ret jrning tram a test a 
lew years 3(0 with a b ufcen nose and Iwd

strokes in their arsenal. Depending 
on the kind of delivery, you can get 
I hem to execute sweeps, on-djiyes. 
off-drives, hooks and plenly morfe. If 
you choose a stroke which is inpos 
sibla to play on the current ball, the 
chances are your batter will just 
stand there looking bashful. 

Bowling is a matter ol being able 
to waggle the joystick extremely fast. 
This is used to determine the speed 
of last bowlers or spin of 
swing balls 

Fortunately your ami 
gets a decent rest between 
overs, so you can put ihe 
Ralgexaway. 

Your fielders are com- 
puter controlled, which is a 
little frustrating as they 
adhere utterly to procedure 
and never lake risks. 

For instance, no matter 
how close they are to 
bowlers-end stumps, they'll 
always throw to the wicket 
keeper, which means its 
possible lo exploit this and 
grab a couple of easy runs, 
The batting and bowting 
orders are initially decided 
by the computer, which 
automatically selects the 
best players in your side 
and places them first, It 



pays to keep an eye on its selection 

as it doesn't take tactics into account 
and will readily give you four fast 
oowlers followed by all your spin 
sowlers. 

Without a doubt the game's most 
outstanding feature is its graphics. 
The animation is superb and the 
players are a decert size. The sound 
effects are sparse, but add to the 
atmosphere. 

At the end of the day there are 
just some things ihis game can't sim- 
ulate, such as the fear generated by 
a lump of leather-wrapped wood 
hurtling towards your head at 
tOOmph, 

rl you can handle that, and are 
prepared to give up the customary 
visit to the ale house at the end ol a 
match, you should find plenty of hjn

GAME REVIEW 





DOMARK £34.99 

After losing the rights to 
screen FA Premier League 
matches on normal' TV, the 
major channels have turned to rugby 
as a way of catching the post- 
Sunday dinner audiences. 

As a result of that and some 
excel lenl showings from the England 
learn, the sport's popularity has 
undergone a meteoric rise to the 
poirt where Joe Public is actually 
interested in friendly matches as well 
as the major tournaments- 
Cashing in on the sport's popular- 
ity. Domark have taken their original 
1991 rugby gams, made a lew minor 
adjustments here and there (and I 
mean minor) and churned it out in 
time to catch the Sevens Work! Cup. 

What's beyond me is why Ihey 
didn't produce a sevens game, 
Rugby Challenge is so similar lo the 
firsl rugby game that there s no point 
in buying it if you own its predeces- 
sor. Basing it on rugby sevens would 
have made sense as it's a tar faster 
game which requires completely dif- 
ferent tactics to its 15-aside cousin. 

For those unfamiliar with ihis 
game, it covers just about every fea- 
ture ol the sport from line-ouls to 
place kicks. The player graphics are 
small, which makes the action con- 
fusing at limes, bul on the whole the 
gameplay is fluid and fast. The action 
changes for kicks, wilh you viewing 
the goal from behind the kicker. To 

M 



A&ovbcT** accuficy of your place *ieh 
depend* on how wKI y*u negoli*<* the wind, 

L*lt: View tt» tel«m »i iridto mode. 

get Ihe bail through the uprights you 

need to stop a steadily increasing 
power bar at the right place by press- 
ing lire. 

To keep you occupied you can 
take your team to ihe world cup, five 
nations tournament or on a tour 
against learns of youf choice. 
There's also a replay feature which 
lets you waieh the best moments ot a 
match over and ever again until 
you're utterly sick of them, 

II you're a die-hard rugby who 
hasn't bought the original, you won't 
go lar wrong here. Even if you're not 
a fan of the sport, ihe Kick Off style 
game play makes tor an excellent 
diversion Irom the usual footy 
games. 

Mark Patterson

MERIT SOFTWARE £44 



Just by reading ihe title oHhis 
game you know you're running 
into trouble. Tom Landry? 

Never heard of him. Strategic? That 
means no action, right? Football? 
Yes, the American kind. 

Avoiding the popular action for- 
mat, Merit have opted to take us 
behind the scenes tor a head 
coach's eye y^ew of the game. Your 
contribution to the team takes place 
en the tactics screen. Here you 
assign what delence or attack they 
are to execute. When this is .done 
you simply sit back and watch the 
players battle it out. WHaf S frustrat- 
ing about this part is that the 
graphics are actually very good, and 
if you were given Ihe opportunity lo 
control the players this would be a 
rather excellent game- 
So what do you do when the 
computer informs you that the 
offence are lining up For a shotgun? 
In my case I selected the likeliest 
looking formation and hoped for the 
best. It worked. I won my lirst match 
against New England 59-3. The 
manual touches on ihe various plays 
suggesting which ones are appropri- 
ate for certain situations, but even 
then it's impossible to 'read' Ihe 
opposition to work out what they're 
planning. 

Watch ine kicfc-ott Irom lha stand*. 





With each quarter lasting t5 min- 
utes, not including the time it takes to 
select a play, boredom soon smacks 
you square between ihe eyes. There 
aren't that many plays, and by the 
feme you tmish a game your 
synapses have evolved to a suffi- 
cient level to actually understand 
them. 

After lhat much play, I'd seen 
enough to convince me lhat I really 
didn't want to play again. It's exceed- 
ingly repetitive - which probably has 
moie to do with the limited nature of 
the game than the subject matter. I 
doubt that even genuine American 
fans of the game would find much 
lasting interest here. 

II Merit were aiming for a man- 
agement game they missed the mark 
by not including translers, dralt 
picks, training, financial difficulties 
and the heart problems which dog 
most 'NFL head coaches. 

This is the kind ot game whose 
ancestors were text-onty titles back 
on the ZX81 . Unfortunately, 
the addition of some nice 
graphics end a decent 
user- interlace doesn't 
cover-up the primitive 
gameplay and a total lack 
of action. You're much bet- 
ter of) sticking with the mix 
ol action and strategy 
offered in EA's excellent . 



Mark Patterson

GAME REVIEW 



CHAMPIONSHIP 

MAN vlii 93 



■ normally despise football man- 
agement games, mainly because 
ihey offer wery little thai the origi- 
nal Football Manager didnl - and 
trial's around 10 years old now. The 
r*ewly revamped Championship 
Manager, however, somehow con- 
vinced me to keep playing, no matter 
how much I tried to hate it. 




firstly it's current, All the Premier 
League teams are featured along 
with the top European dubs. Each 

team has the same players as the 
real-life squads, with statistics reflect- 
ing their current form. This is 
important to football freaks like me 
who cant stand managing teams 
with players who were eligible for the 
'66 World Cup squad. 

When you take the seat at your 
new desk your first job is to organise 
the players. As with most games ol 
this type they're given ratings for 
attributes such as passing, shooting, 
tackling and speed. They also have 
contracts, disputes with Itie manager 
' other players and generally 
unpredictable traits. At this 
point you can afford to flog the 
trouble makers and invest your 
cash in some rising Stars 

RESERVISTS 

Another feature which I haven't 
seen before is the inclusion of 
reserve teams. Where the first 
team players have ratings tor 
their skills, clicking on a 
reserve piayer just gives you a 
comment such as 'promising' 
or lair . Using one of these is 
I risky, but if you're hit with an 
j injury crisis it's usually the only 
place to turn 

The transler market is Ihe 
best place to pick up new tal- 
ent. Amateur managers might 
just scout ihe market and see if 
there's anything they like, while 
the pros employ scouts. This 
motley crew have proficiency 
ratings depending on how suc- 
cessful they are, All you need 
to do is tell them what position 



skillu! they should be, which divisions 
to look in and what age range to look 
at. They'll obligingly go away and a 
week later report back with a list of 
every player who tils the bill, whether 
they're transfer listed or unhappy at 
their current club. 

The team's tactics have to be set 
before a match rl you don't want your 
players to end up milling arounc i tea 
lost sheep. This feature goes into 
some depth, as you have to assign 
each player to the position they're 
best suited to. choose the formation, 
style of play and whether c not your 
team hold back or push forward. The 
game styles range Iron continental 
to long- ball and there's just about 




Relate the sea hki atari* hi aarrwat you Cam 
se1 up a couple ut IHandly milch**. Here We»( 
Ham are cannon 1o4d*r far 1h* Gunner*. 



every formation you could ask for. 
Setting the tactics is extremely 
time consuming to begin- with and ini- 
tially left me wondering rf I'toutd be 

bcthcfod continuing with a full sea- 
son. Fortunately the game stores 
your last set of tactics, so once 
you've found a winning game plan 
you rarely need to alter it 

As you can Jell from the screen- 
shots on this page, there's not much 
to look at in this game. There are a 
number of preset digitised back- 



Move over, Brian 
Clough - Mark 
Patterson puts on 
his manager's cap 
and gives it one 
hundred per cent.

GENERAL TIPS 

• If you're attacking coastat posi- 
tions, approach them from tfie sea 
as you don't use any luel over 
water, so you can take your lime. 

• It's best to uncover as much 
hidden fuel and ammo as possible 
early In each campaign so you 
know where to go in an emer- 
gency. 

• Don't waste your valuable mis* 
siles on inanimate objects - use 
your chain gun if you're not in a 
hurry. 

• Remember thai some objects 
such as buildings are neutral and 
are not automatically targeted. 
You may have to jink your heli- 
copter to Nne up the shot. 

• There are lots of Armour Repair 
Toolboxes scattered about each 
level, especially in the later cam- 
paigns. There is always one 
secreted beneath a power station 

your chain gun, then fire your mis- 
siles at the tanks. Alter you've 
secured Ihe area, fire your chain 
gun at the door and jink around and 
listen until you hear that your shols 
are hitting home, 

Your goal in mission four is to 
destroy five of the six garbage 
trucks carrying nuclear bomb parts. 
Do not destroy any of Ihe four 
trucks carrying innocent civilians - 
you are only allowed 10 make one 
mistake! To determine which trucks 
are carrying nuclear material, you 
must see the payload in the rear, if 
the truck is driving at an angle that 
does not allow you to see the pay 
load, wait until it turns in another 
direction, The nuclear payload 
looks like canisters. 

The final mission involves a race 
against lime lo stop general 
Kilbaba launching a nuclear 
bomber. Unfortunately, the plane 
comes with a hefty 3000 armour 
points, so fire at will with all you've 
got! You've only got about thirty 
seconds before the bomber 
reaches ihe end of the runway, so 
good lucki

THE ADDAMS FAMILY 

MIT SQUAD OUT HOW £9.99 

Those creepy and kooky members of The Addams Fatuity are the stare or this 
Mario-inspired platform romp, Taking control of a moustachioed Gomez 
Sprite, it's up to you to guide the Fez-wearing philanderer around a series of 
platform-packed levels in a frantic bid to rescue his kidnapped wife and chil 
dren Apparently, a ruthless property dealer has spirited away Mortiei-a, Fesler 
and ihe rest of the Addams elan and is holding them to ransom until Gomez 
signs over the deeds 10 his lamily home 

Each character has been hidden in a different section of the sprawling 
family mansion, so Gomez must visit all live wings of the house to secure 
their release. Its not as simple as that, though, as each leve ' is stuffed to ttte 
gills with all manner ol marauding nasties and end-ot-level guardians and 
these can only be defeated by bouncing repeatedly an their bonces, Fail to 
pull off this tricky manoeuvre and you'll more than likely lose some much 
needed energy and eventually forfeit a life. As you only start the game with 
five lives, its best to time your moves to perfection, otherwise you'll suffer the 
consequences. 

The Gomez sprite can be made la perform all kinds of acrobatic twists and 
turns at the tug of a joystick, with the fire button used to propel him through 
the air. He's every bit as manoeuvrable as Nintendo's Mario character and 
there's even a Fez-copter to reach some of the game's more inaccessible 
areas The in-game graphics are striking enough, although some of the back- 
grounds are a littie lacklustre, and the music is a suitably jolly piece. Where 
the game falls down is in the collision detection. You can be nowhere near an 
outcropping spike or incoming canncnball and yet still get zapped. That's a 
great pity as it ruins an otherwise excellent game 

Of course, there are all the usual bonus rooms and hidden stages you'd 
expect in a game of this nature, and most are stuffed lo the gills with power- 
ups and life-giving tokens Each level Is also based on a particular theme, so 
you've got a garden siage populated with carrot-crunching kilter rabbits and a 
huge egg-bombing bird and a kitchen level which features an army gf knHe- 
wiekding chefs When the game was released a year ago, it scooped up 
awards by the truck load, but things look a little jaded now and there are more 
polished contenders for your cash

Tony 'horrid noises a speciality' Horgan takes a cou 
pie of new sample CDs for 
THE WIZARD 



a spin. 
GROOVE CONSTRUCTION 



19 was a milestone in dance music. Paul 
Hardcastle's revolutionary use ol the sampler 
pushed it to number one m the world's charts, 
and started (he ball rolling for the sample-based 
dance explosion or the late 90s. Compiled from 
Mr Hardcastle's own sample library. The Wizard 
is another of those very handy all-rounder sam- 
pie discs. 

Techno loops are first on Ihe menu, There are 
41 of these, which have been: composed especially 
for the CD. not taken from other records. Most are 
based on single-bar rhythms played on analogue 
synths, often looped a number of times with 
increasing degrees ol modulation and filter levels. 
Put these on a thumping beat, maybe add some 
chords if you're feeling adventurous, and you've 
got yourself the basis of a good fartingly-slnhenng 
techno track. Some otthe loops are based on the 
same ntfs, but played on different sounds, so you 
don't quite gel 41 completely different loops, 

Next are 27 keyboard riffs and drop-ins, fol- 
lowed by a rather excessive but comprehensive 
orchestral section. A dozen basslines put in an 
appearance, again with a good squidgy analogue 
flavour, some repealed on different sounds to 
make up the numbers. A batch ol 3? breakbeats is 
followed by 1 percussion loops, which lead into 
some single drum samples. 

The speech section is a little disappointing, 
with recreated versions of some dated samples. 
The vocal ad libs are a tot more useful. Even bet- 
ter are the sax and flute riffs, which have bajgs of 
potential for catchy hooks and fills. Various olher 
sounds include synth samples, kijng fu vocals 
guitar riffs and notes, and single bass sam pies. ' 
Snippets from 19 Often crop up, including the 19 
sample itself. 

Even though some of the sounds are a little out 
of tune with current trends, the variety means you'll 
be sampling the CD for many months to come. If 
you're into loops, I his is one of the best CDs 
around - full of bread and butter samples, with 
plenty of jam dolloped on lop. 
Prlce: £49.95 



Another new concept Irom Time and Space, 

Groove Construction is an alternative breakbeat 
CD, Instead of taking drum loops from existing 
records. MJ Dunne has created them all himself. 
With the CD, you also get a 3-5 inch floppy disk 
containing MIDI files of Ihe loops on the CD. The 
idea is that you can either sample the loops 
straight irom the CD, or recreate them with the 
MIDI files and the separate drum samples also 
included on the CD. 

The MIDI files are in ST format, so you"ll need 
to convert them to AmigaDos first with something 
like the PD programs MessyDos Of MessySlD, 
You Could also use them with OclaMED, by first 
getting them into AmigaDos, and then using the 
AMFC conversion program from Amiganuts [0703 
470017). Once you've got them in your sequencer, 
you can rearrange them to suit your needs, or use 
the rhythms with your own choice of samples. If 
you want to keep your sample polyphony tor other 
sounds, you could easily route the loops through a 
drum machine. 

Whatever k rid ol dance music you're into, 
there's something tor you on the CD. The disc is 
split into categories for EurorTechno. Swing/New 
Jack. Garage, House, Hip Hop/Rap, and 
Hardcore/Rave. All of them are built from separate 
drum and sound effect samples, so none are real 
live breakbeats, but the sound quality is very high. 
It's interesting to hear someone else's idea of what 
garage, techno and hip hip are about, The hard- 
core loops for example, aren't whal I would think of 
as typical examples of hardcore, but then if you 
want a typical hardcore breakbeat, you can sample 
one Irom a typical hardcore record. 

Qrpove Construction shouldn't be viewed as a 
normal drumtoop CD, H that's what you want, 
ypu'd be better off looking elsewhere. However, if 
you want more freedom than single bar dnjmtoopS 
give you, or it you're looking to improve your own 
groove-creation skills, this could be right up your 
alley, The addition of the MIDI files is an excellent 
idea, and one that deserves to succeed, 




Both available from-. Time and Space, 

PO Box 306, Berkhamstead, Herts, 

HP4 3EP, Teh 0442 870631.

HYPERCACHE CDTV MEMORY SWITCH 






If you have a need for 
speed, perhaps you 
should be swapping your 
cash for a cache. 

The one thing that everyone knows about mass 
Storage devices is Ihat they are never fast 
enough. CD-ROM. . pah! You may gel 600Mb 
of storage on line, but what good is it to you it it takes 
an age to read. Even the performance of SCSI hard- 
drives comes into question when performing 
demanding tasks. 

Hypercache is a small piece 1 of software with a sin- 
gle goal - to speed up all your data acquisition 
without getting in the way of any other software. H 
works by setting aside some memory as a bufter to 
your storage device. When the same fite intorma&on, 
lor example a directory list, is requested often the 
data can be supplied direct from this buffer without 
the need to read the disk. Hypercache uses an LRU 
algorithm, derived from those used on large main- 
Irame and minicomputer fileservers. 

Hypercache wiH run on virtually any Amiga, It will 
cope equaJly well with WB1 ,2, WB1 3, WB2 and WB3 
and there is even a special optimised version for '030 
and 040 users. There is no memory requirement, 
although at least 1 Mb is recommended to gel the best 
out of the software The default cache sue is 512K, 

A small piece of cunning added to trie program 
even pre-loads information that it predicts you are 
going to ask for, based on previous esperience. The 
software >s fully user-configurable, allowing you to 
fiddle about with all the parameters and muck 
things up. More importantly you can change me 
size of the buffer (which defaults to 5l2k) to better 
reflect your needs 

The system as a whole will be useful to everyone 
who uses software like ray-tracers, databases, anima- 
tions, compilers or even just Workbench 

Available from: Silicon Prairie Software, 2326 
Francis Street, Regina, 5K S*M 2P7- Tel: 0101 
306 362 0358- Rice $49,95 (money order ^^^ 
in US currency accepted }, Price f^?S^ 

includes overseas P&P- \^^j 



Tony Horgan takes a trip down memory lane. 

If you've got a 1Mb CDTV, you've probably realised by now that you can't actually use the full 
megabyte. The CD drive uses a buffer of about 5QK to keep the access times as low as possible, 
but it takes this 50K from your 1Mb of RAM. This isn't too much of a problem in most cases, but 
some programs need the wtiole megabyte to run properly. This is the cause of virtually all CDTV 
incompatibility problems. 

The switch simply disables the CD drive, and reclaims thai extra 50K. Installing it is quite straightfor- 
ward, but you have to take the case off the CDTV, which will void your warranty. A diagram is included 
with the instructions lo help you locale the jumper on the circuit board. There's no neat way of passing 
the wire through the casing of the CDTV, but il works fine if you put rl through the gap around the PCM- 
CIA slot at the front (although it does look a bit messy), 

It works perfectly, but if the machine breaks down, it'll be up 10 you to get it fixed. At £12.99 its a bit 
pricey, considering the parts can't cost more than about £2. II you fancy a cheaper alternative, take a 
look at the software option in this issue's PO Utilities section. 

Available tram: CD Free, 48 Lindisfame Road, Spennymoor. Co Durham, DL16 6E1. Tel: (%*^fc 
03Saei366t. Price: El 2.99. v*^j

CYBERDREAM 

Tony Horgan puts on his slippers and relaxes with a 




cup of Horlicks and a good video. 




■ ow do you fancy a bit of progressive, uplifting, spiritual house', combined with an eyeful of 
■■ trarce-induring visuals? Weed a break from all lhat head-banging techno? Then get your sensory 

1 organs around Cyberdream. 


»^W1 


■1p% 


Whether you want to wind down after a night out, get yourself in the mood for a boogie, or if you ve 


***■ & *%y* 


rust had enough of News at Ten. Cyberdream is the answer. It's a 20- minute vtdeo of colour -cycling Irac- 
tats, kaleidoscopic warps, abstract animations and 3D spinny things (Stop me if I'm getting too technical}. 
My personal lavourite is the psychedelic rainbow-coloured shark. The backing is a weH-surled mix of what 
many would call progressive house, but that reminds me oi those awful progressive rock bands of the 
70s. so we'll call it trance instead. In of her words, wooshy synths, blobby basses and simple melodies 
over grooving house beats - most satisfactory. 

The sound and graphics combine really well, and you don't have to be consciously watching or listen- 
ing to gel the benefit. Think of it as you would an EP or an album, or even as a moving painting. Then 
again, maybe you should think of it as a freaky v«feo.AH the graphics were done with an Amiga which, in 
a way. makes it even more impressive. See this month's Lead Feature for more info on the creators At

Flatulartt bottom end? A 
bit fuzzy up top? Dr Tony 
Horgan takes a look at a 
miracle cure. 

If you ask me, designing the Amiga's sound 
hardware was the best thing Commodore have 
ever done. The fi-bit resolution is out-dated 
these days, but it's still capable of producing some 
killer tunes. The trouble is, in theory, the output 
should be better than it actually is, 

If you've ever sampled a loop and played it 
back to back with the original, you'll almost cer- 
tain ly notice a drop in the top-end frequencies, no 
matter how well you've recorded it. According 10 
the laws of logic, it you sample at or above the 
Nyquist rate, all 1 the high frequencies should come 
through in your recording. Even Ihough this is usu- 
ally the case, when it comes to playback, some of 
the higher frequencies are suppressed- 



One way around the problem is to use software to 
boost the treble. This works well with some sam- 
ples, but if your sound has been recorded with an 
optimum signal to noise rato, the treble could dis- 
lort when it's amplified. Another alternative is to 
tweak the controls on your graphic equaliser, but 
many EQs don't provide sufficient levels of cut and 
boost to make much difference. 

The new Sound Enhancer from Omega Projects 
i$ what you need- The original Enhancer was a 
basic treble booster, but the improved version is 
more impressive. Don't be deceived by its modest 
appearance - the difference if makes is stunning - 
you get a far brighter sound, with added wellie at 
the bass end, On top of that, the EQ also brings 
out various other frequencies lhal are usually lost 



The only controls on the unit are a knob and a 
switch. The switch turns the Enhancer effect on 
and off. while the knob controls the amount of tre- 
ble boost, so you can alter it to suit your particular 
hi-fi setup You can't adjust the level of bass 
boost, but you can easily strike a good overall bal- 
ance by tweaking the treble knob and the voiurne 
on your hi-fi. 

If your samples are grainy in the first place, the 
Enhancer will make ffiem sound worse For exam- 
ple, drum loops recorded below about 16KHz will 
sound scratchy, and background noise will 
become more apparent on quiet samples. The tre- 
ble boost is centred around the 20KHz range, and 
any frequencies of that pitch in a 16KHz sample 
will be distorted. The Sound Enhancer will jusl 
emphasise them. On the other hand, decent sam- 
ples take on a new dimension. Play a sample with 
the Enhancer switched out. then switch it in. and 
and the sound leaps right out of the speakers at 



JARGON BUSTERS 



• HVQuiST RATE - Tttt rale a( wDicti a sound must be 
recorded In urder Id tap hi re tile highest frequencies of lilt 
ori(i nal. This rale ft ttuce lite freq jeney o! the highest 
pilcti in Itie sound. 

• EO - Snort tor equalisation: the adjustment At relative 
frequencies within 4 sound. 

• SIC NAL-TO-NQISE RATIO - Ttiej level of rewiM sound 
relative to Ike background noise. Higher ratios give supe- 
rior feprpitueliuns. 

• dB - Decibels, the accepted ilwdird lor manuring val- 
: time levels 




Enhancer 



you ! It's like the difference between a mono cas- 
sette tape, and a surround sound CD (although of 
course it's not CO quality) 

If you've ever tried to record your music to ana- 
logue tape, you'll know what a struggle it is to get 
your recording to sound anything like the original. 
Most Amigas have a low-rated output that only reg- 
isters about - 5dB. it you put that straight onto tape 
(via your hi-fi "s CD inputs lor example), you'll have 
a very poor signai-lo-noise ratio, and you'll end up 
with a bad recording as a consequence. Passed 
through the Sound Enhancer, the level is boosted 
10 about +3dfl. which is just about the highest level 
a cassette can take belore distorting. With a higher 
volume level on the tape, there's not so much room 
for all that horrible hiss and hum to creep in. Not 
only will the overall level be right, Put you'll also 
have control over the treble (treble response varies 
a great deal depending on the type ot cassette, 
tape deck and noise reduction you're using). 

CONCLUSION 

In shofl. anything you record from the Amiga will 
sound much better if it's gone through the Sound 
Enhancer first.. Even if the sample rates are low, 
you can still benelil from the extra meal of the bass 
boost, and keep the treble control turned down, 
Also, don't forget that this doesn't have to replace 
any existing equalisers you might have. There's no 
reason why you can't put your sound through as 
many EOs as you can like in order to hone your 
music to perfection .fgtj

The race is on to produce a paint 
package capable of doing justice lo 
the AGA Amigas. Yes, there is a 
special version on DP&int, and there is 
Personal Paint, but bolti of these suffer 
Irom similar drawbacks Although they 
support the new screen and colour 
modes, the -esults slill lall short of photo 
graphic quality- 
It isn't a great feat to compose an 
image in HAMS mode. What is difficult is 
keeping it looking good. Normally any HAMS 
images you see on the AGA machines in demos 
and the like have been converted down Irom a 24- 
brt Me. When you start mucking around with the 
picture, it starts looking tarty because all the 
effects, such as antialiasing, are taking place in 
eight brtpianes. The reason ttiat images processed 
in packages like ADPro look niuch better, is 
because they operate on 24-bit dala Even it some 
of that information is losl when it is converted 
down, you started out with better ingredients. 




W I P 




Th» mom impreMiwe feature ot Tru*p»int tit* to b* mt very ettecliv* 
patoue option a. 



There is a speed loss involved in deal- 
ing with 24-bit graphics - but it is not all 
that different lo the amount of processing 
time required for HA MB images. ,- 

• On running the software, a requestor 
will ask which screen mode to run in. 
J Memory permitting, a speed-up buffer will 
be initialised al this stage. The resolutions 
provided include the overscan range, so 
TfuePatnt could be used tor real-time 
genlocKing, even in AGA mode. Image 
size is up to 16366 by 16368 pixels, although obvi- 
ously you can't display all these on screen at once. 
The interface for I he program will be familiar to 
anyone who read the exclusive Retina review we 
ran in our April issue. Essentially the program is 
the same as the bundled VDPaint software, except 
il runs through Workbench instead oi directly onto 
a 24-bit card, The only major difference is Itiat the 
display updates are marginally slower, mainly 
because the Retina had its own exceptionally last 
memory and the standard Amiga doesn't. 

Obviously though, the fact that it is not run- 
ning on a pure 24-bil board makes some 
difference lo some ol the features. 



TOOLS 

Instead of tbe tool bar associated with most 
paint packages [DPaint, Photon Paint, 
Personal Paint etc). TruePaini has a tool 

panel, which pops up in the mtddle of the 
screen whenever the menu button is pressed. 
All actions, including file handling, prefer- 
ences and exiting the software are handled 
from this panel. This may not conform rigor- 
ously, or mdeed at all, to the Commodore 
Style Guide, but perhaps in lhis instance it is 
excusable. 

The most impressive features ot TruePaint 
are immediately obvious when you've had a 
bit of a fiddle. Perhaps the most astounding 
feature is the Airbrush which actually 



Left Truapalnt will in1*rl»c* dir.clly w|]h V-Lab. lha popularly 

acclaimed digitl&er Irom Macr oSyaiem

behaves like an airbrush. The spray Irom the noz- 
zle can be edited in a value-profile selection. 

The tools and features all behave in a compos- 
ite hierarchical structure. If you select the airbrush 
and the circle *ool. you will draw circles with an air- 
brush and so on, Transparency curves can also 
apply lo brushes, which is very handy tor overlay 
effects 

There are an impressive number of palette edit- 
ing and cycling tools, giving you full control over 
I hie extended colour ranges available to the 
machine. What's mere ihey are handled m a read- 
ily accessible manner which will be familiar to 
artists on many professional graphics systems 

Support lor JPEG and full AFtexx support 
means that this paint package is well sel to take on 
the future. JPEG is da nigeurfor the larger than 
average files created by AGA images. The Unis 
PPM variant is also supported for some obscure 
reason. 

CONCLUSION 

Judging by this demo, this is tfie most impressively 
featured AGA paint package yet, although the 
interface may not be quite as easy to use as the 
likes of DPaint, Nevertheless, given the remarkable 
airbrush tool, font Support and multiple undo facili- 
ties, il may be thai for presentation Desktop 
graphics. TruePaint will take off in a big way. 
It does require a lot ol resources to get going 
though. The basic setup would be an AGA 
machine with 2Mb of graphics memory and WB3 Q 
Or above - in Other words an A 1200 - but only just. 
You will soon find that 4Mb is a more reasonable 
amount of memory and that a hard drive is a bit of 
a necessity.

Teaches Typing II 



Nick Veitch attempts to 
find out whether ten fin- 
gers are better than two. 

T 1 here are some supreme achievements that 
have marked man's progress through the 
swirls of history: the development of lan- 
guage, the construction of the Great Pyramid. 
powered flighl. rockets landing on the moon and 
PSA standard typing. You may prioritise Ibese in 
some other way, but lor me the ability to knock out 
over 100 words a minute on a keyboard is akin to 
climbing Everest- Apparently though, it is nol a 
prime requisite lo sell your soul to the devil. It 
seems that ordinary people can achieve this 
Herculean feat without resort to pentagrams and 
incense. All you need is a bit ol training 



M8tVl8 Beacon is well known, we understand, tor 
her amazing typing ability Well, maybe she is, but 
al CU Towers we only remember her from the orig- 
inal Mavis Beacon program. Anyway, this is a 
structured typing tutorial, designed by an expert 
The approach is quite entertaining, There are

several modules to work through and you may 
start al one of three skill levels Firstly there is a*, 
section which simply tesls you on your speed. Text 
appears on the screen which you have to follow In 
order lo give you a bit of a hand there is a set ol " ■ 
ghost hands which appears over a keyboard in the 
bottom half of ihe window. At the end of about tOO 
words ol text you are given a breakdown o( your 
performance. 

As well as a pure speed in words per minute 
there is also an accuracy tesl which gives an effec- 
tive WPM with deductions for the errors. These 
lessons will introduce new combinations of letters ' 
lo drive the user forward in terms of flexibility At 
the novice level these sesswjns will be the on&s 
which introduce new characters. 

II a mistake is made in this mode it should be 
corrected. Mistakes are allowed to stand, but if you 
make an error with a space it can throw oul ihe 
whole rest of the sentence being typed. Other 
training modes will simply not accept incorrect 
keystrokes {but they will tot them up and use them 
against you when the session is over). 

GAMES 

There are two games in the software which pro 
vide a fun aspect to learning. In ihe typing tutor 
you get to race a car. in order to keep in front you 
must type in the words which scroll across the Sky 
in front of you. 

The opponent's car will go at a steady speed 
but you wil often find your own vehicle surging 
ahead and suddenly dying as you come across 
familiar words and phrases Mistakes will cause 
some rather grotesque smeanng experiences 
involving the windscreen and a member ol the 
local insect population. 

The keypad game is a Irttte harder. Hems roll 
along a simulated checkout till towards a snooping

bag. As they pass over Ihe scanner a price will 
flash up which has lo be entered before the cab- 
bage or whatever continues its journey. 
Unfortunately, even in complete novice mode, the 
length of time allowed is very small This game isn't 
quite as exerting. 

CONCLUSION 

Mavis attempts to make the lessens interesting by 
varying the text and inlerspersing the new material 
with lun games. The games themselves are a bit 
primitive , but ft is a change from just repetitive typ- 
ing exercises. Each of the different styles of lesson 
incorporates all the classic devices of typing 
courses so there is no doubl as to ihe academic 
credentials of this package. 

As with all training software though, it is onfy 
effective il the user really wants lo learn, Mavis 
Beacon may be significantly more interesting than 
more traditional education packages, but it slill 
doesn't beat a game of Sensible Soccer. It does 
look a bit loo much like a PC port, but then the PC 
version is £70 more expensive, so maybe you 
should count yourself lucky. j^J>
