If the phrase *3-in-1 ' 
makes you think of light 
machine oil, perhaps 
you'd better take a look 
at Interactivision's compi- 
lation. Nick Veitch did. 

The idea ol an integrated soltware package 
probably started with Microsoft Works on ihe 
IBM PC. The idea is a terribly simple one. If 
you want to increase efficiency and productivity, all 
your software should be similar and work in similar 
ways, using Ihe same shortcuts for functions which 
are common to all applications and so on. 

Microsoft developed the idea further to ease the 
transfer of data between two applications. In this 
sense InterOftiCQ is not, strictly speaking, an inte- 
grated package. There are three separate utilities, 
which can co-operate in some oases, but which 
don't share any common data area. 

ft does not compete directly with the likes of 
GokJ Disk Otttca or MintQtiice in lerms of Ihe porta- 
bility of data, and it is much easier to consider it as 
a bumper pack ol three separate applications 



Probably the most common computer application 
known to man, the word processor, is also most 
likely to be the application users spend the majonly 
of their time looking at ft is important than, that the 
controls should be easy to use and ihe display 

Thm B the prettiest part of the whole p*ck*Qe- 




pleasant to look at 

The menu options include some features miss- 
ing on far more expensive pieces of software. The 
shortcuts are all on tairty sensible keys, although 
the Mac standard of X. C & V has been used for 
cut and paste operations 

Multiple documents are supported, rather in the 
same way that Protext supports them, in that they 
do not appear as separate windows, but you can 
page between them or have a top and bottom' dis- 
play. Interword is one of those text processors 
which likes to orientate towards how it will look 
when output. It is a What You See Is Almost What 
You Get package, because it does show indents, 
bold and italic lest, headers, etc bul only makes 
provision for raw text - there's none of fhat fooling 
around with different fonts, 



A database is important to any business but good 
ones are rare on the Amiga. This is really the acid 
test for any integrated Amiga package. 

Inlerbase refers to a database of objects as a 
table, each table consisting of a number of differ- 
ent fields. All the fields must be entered before any 
actual information is added. The fields can toe of 
many different types to cater tor numeric, alphanu- 
meric and date/time data. If is also possible lo 
select various error trapping cohorts at this stage. 

Entering the data is fust a matter of typing it in 
on screen. This can be a time consuming task and 
is not really helped by having to keep selecting 
'add a new record' every time. Most database pro- 
grams these days have an automatic entry system, 
meaning you can just type away until you run out 
of tilings to enter. 

The facilities offered are, like the word-pro. 
fairly advanced, but the major lei-down is the dis- 
play, Which is very unnattractrve and quite 
contusing. All Ihe data is simply listed with one 
record following after another 



The spreadsheet is the star of this collection, it 
may be as visually deficient as Ihe other programs. 
but ihe features more than make up for this, Unlike 
many newer and ostensibly more professional 
packages, Inrerspread can handle as many rows 
and columns as you like. Many Amiga spread- 
sheets will only allow around a hundred columns of 
data, which is msuff icieni tor those who like to use 
their spreadsheets as inventory records or similar 
applications. 



There is a reasonable set of formulae available, 
and the ability to program in macros for specific 
repetitive tasks Information can be block copied 
around the worksheet and exported directly to the 
word-pro. which makes compiling reports easier. 

The graph drawing facilities support eight differ- 
ent lypes of chart, which are all displayed on a 
seperale screen in up lo eight colours The charts 
may be printed ou| directly or saved as IFF files. 
Essentially this module has a feature list similar to 
that ol MaxiPian reviewed in February. 



All the progamic will run on 1.3 or better versions of 
Workbench, but this is no way justifies the rather 
old-style feel to the program, ft was certainly 
designed with 1 3 in mind, with only a small range 
of screen options and a 4-colour limit, It would be a 
shame to run any of these programs on an AG A 

machine. 

Having said that they all, in their individual way, 
have at teast one useful feature not found any- 
where else. /Zn

Learning to read can be a hard experience lor children. After all, with S2 
shapes to remember you need all the help you can get. Mickey's ABC's is 
designed lo help preschool children (2-5) recognise letters and sounds 
through an imaginative cartoon interface. The way it works is simple. There 
are two main scenes - Mickey's home and the local fair. For bolh scenes, 
there are 26 things lor Mickey to do, including travel between: the two, and 
each thing begins with a different letter. Typing a letter tarings up a short ani- 
mation of Mickey and friends acting out the action, along with verbal 
commentary ot what they are doing, 

Essentially, there are two ways to play Mtck9ys ABC's. The first is just to 
play around, hitting a key at random and watching what Mickey does. 
Perhaps not high on educational value, but it does help the child gel used lo 
the interface, 
Secondly, ygu 
can point some- 
thing out to a 
child - an ice 
cream van for 
example - and. 
see if they can 
get Mickey to eat 
some ice cream. 
Naturally the key 
to type isT, and 
getting the child 
to recognise this 
fact is a signifi- 
cant stage in the 
learning process. 

The presentation is superb. Full cartoon animation abounds, aiong with 
stacks and stacks of samples of both Mickey and other Disney characters. 
and a friendly tomato voice-over that explains what's happening. Type the 
letter T while Mickey is at home, and the voice will say T - as in Television', 
the word 'Television 1 15 displayed on screen and Mickey will wander off to 
the sitting room and switch on the box 

My only real criticism of this otherwise excellent product is the loading 
times. Due to the massive amounts of sampling used, the program has to 
access the drive every time you do something, resulting in a slow moving 
game. This could iead to frustrating gamepiay. After ait, yau and I know 
what's happening when the game Ireezes and the drive light comes 
on, but whose lo say that a three year old does 7 Otherwise, a 
product that fulfils all it sets out lo do.

There's a lot more to numbers than two plus two, and this is something that 
most educational packages tend to miss out Sure, there are more than 
enough edutainment products dedicated to helping your children to count from 
one to a hundred and handle ah sorts of operations on the figures between, 
but lhafs all. Mickey's 123's - subtitled The Big Surprise Party - takes bask; 
numbers (0 to &) and uses them as part of a semi-planning, semi -recognition 
operation. 

The game tells ol Mickey's plans to throw a surprise party for a random 
character. Wbat yo\j have to do is go to the toy factory, create a present tor 
the intended surprisee, invite your guests and then buy the food and decora- 
tions. Travel around Mickey's small town is via the numeric keypad, with each 
destination numbered, 

At the toy lactory. basic number recognition is practised, with the child first

bered list. Finally, they have to go shopping, and this is where the planning 
comes into the garrn Enough food needs to be bought to satisfy the guests, 
or the party will be a failure 1 

Again, I he presentation is excellent. AH of the characters are true to their 
cartoon counterparts, and clever use of samples make them even more recog- 
nisable. Once more, though, the loading times slow things down heavily 
Unfortunately this game really relies on keeping up concentration, and with 
everything slopping and starting every few seconds, it might just ^^—pjw

This is a game that all of us have played at some poml under one name or 
another - Pairs, Patience or any other rifle It you haven't recognised it from 
the screenshots. then here's the gist. A set of cards are laid face down. The 
spread contains a set number of pairs and a couple of wild cards. Two players 
lake turns to turn over two cards hoping to find two that match. If a pair is 
revealed, those twc cards are removed from the board and the player turns 
over anolher two cards. If no pair is found, the cards are lurried face down 
again and play hands over to the next player, The aim is to find more pairs 
than the oiher player by remembering ihe positions of previously revealed 
cards. Remember it now?

Moving into the five and above age range comes Mickey's Jigsaw Puzzles, 
an exercise in shape recognition. As you have probably guessed Iron ihe 
title, this game alKjwS you to assemble jigsaws on screen from 1 5 different 
pictures. Choose the image you want to break down, along with the number 
of pieces to break u into and lei the computer do the rest. 

Mickey himself talks you ih rough the entire process, offering help here 
and there and Letting you know the resuil of any options you may have cho- 
sen. This is just another example of Ihe work that has gone into making the 
child feel that Mickey is right there working with them, ralherthan letting ihe 
parent leel they have been conned into another tenuously licensed product. 

From this poini on, all you are left with is a drawing showing the shapes of 
ihe pieces, and the pieces themselves You already know what a jigsaw is, 
so I don"! need lo explain what the player has to do. And. that s it? Yep, it 
seems to be I must admit, it doesn't seem like a hell of a lot. especially when 
you consider mat (a) the 15 pictures included are rhe only ones you can use 
(the program doesn't let you load m piclures of your own, which seems like a 
strange Ihing to omit) and (bi a very similar package <s included as pari of 
Euro-press' Paint and Create, rather than as a standalone product Itke this. 
To be fair, though, Ihis does have a few eitcellenl options. 

Firstly, you can prinl out the pictures as a colouring book. Secondly, there 
are two jigsaw modes. The first is when Ihe entire picture is broken up. like 
any other jigsaw, The second is when a section of the picture, such as 
Mickey himself is removed and broken up, and the player has to fit the 
pieces back nto the original image 

Thirdly, the pictures can be animated once the jigsaw has been com- 
pleted, giving the user an added goal to work toward. These are all solid plus 
points, but on the bottom firm, l don't think (hat Ihere is enough in Jj^jk 
this package to validate the price point. %^jfp

Tony Morgan takes time

Oh no! We've printed the Alan ST screen- 
shots by mistake! Actually, these are the 
Amiga she-Is, believe it or not- The curse of 
the ST-port is back from the grave lo haunt us 
Aniga users, hut is il necessarily such a bad 
thing? After all, until recently. Ihe ST has been way 
out in Iron! as far as MIDI sequencers go. 

Rave isn't trying lo beat Bars and Pipes or 
KCS. Not everyone wants a list of leatures that 
reads like a telephone directory. Why should you 
pay £300 for a piece of software when you know 
you're never going to use half of rl? That's the 
thinking behind Rave, which at £50 is a good deal 
cheaper than any olher MIDI sequencer currency 
in production {except for OctaMED, of course). The 
name suggests that it's- designed with dance music 
in mind, and indeed that does seem to be ihe case. 
However it's still set m many of Ihe ways or the 
traditional sequencer. 



Right- Uucot c*n u« 1he 
nutation «Mor. 



* * 1* 



I 



II drops a massive clanger lor starters, with its 
sample support- Dance music is ail about samples. 

and the Amiga has great sampling abilities, so you 
could reasonably expect lo be able to use your 



sample library with Rave. 
Due to some appalling 
programming practices, 
Rave is only capable of 
playing one sample at a 

time. The manual gives an excuse about this being 
'for reasons of speed', even though the "Amiga is 
quite capable of handling multiple MIDI channels 
and Hour samples simultaneously. If you have IFF 
samples saved with loops, Ihe program refuses to 
load them, with no further explanation than 'a disk 
or dnve error has occurred'. IFFs without loops and 
RAW samples load okay. 

A lew welt chosen samples are included with 
the program, along with some ageing Art" of Noise 
instrument sounds. Unfortunately, the loops have 
dead sections of noise al the start, which causes 
them to run out of sync with: any other sequenced 
music. These people are in the business of making 
music on computers - surety among their ranks 
tbey can find someone competent enough to sam- 
ple a breakbeat^

For precise editing ol your sequences, there's 
Ihe Event Editor. Once again, this is a vertically- 
scrolling window, but is completely texl-orienlated. 
Each 'event' in the sequence is listed in order from 
lop to bottom. Every 'note On' has its own values 
lor volume, velocily, aftertouch, pitchbend, modu-

Another alternative is to use the noiation 
screen. It's possible to perform edits from here, but 
you don't gel Ihe accuracy that's available from the 
event and step editors. There's also an option lo 
output the score to an Epson-compatible printer.

USER-HOSTILE 

On the subject of Ihe general running of the pro-

and alt, while the software

CU AMIGA 



INTERVIEW 





In a surprise move former 

CUM boss Kelly Sumner 

has left to take up the 

challenge of running a 

software company. CU's 

intrepid reporters went off 

to give his replacement, 

Dave Pleasance, a 

grilling. 




ave Pleasance has been at Commodore for 
10 years. He was originally recruited to sell 
PCs, but three months into the job the project 
was abandoned- FortunaMy Dave continued m sates 
and along the way thought up the Batman pack For 
the Amiga, which broke new ground in co-operation 
between compgier manufacturers, software houses 
and Hollywood. His philosophy was emodied in the 
phrase, often used in the adverts at the day: p we 
don't sell computers, we sell dreams," 

In July 1990 he took over as general Manager of 
Commodore Electronics Limited, which markets 
Commodore technology to all the parts of toe world 
which do not have I heir own subsidiaries. After two 
years, in Apnf '92 he became Vice President of con- 
sumer sales in the U.S. 

Returning to the UK, he re -inherited the interna- 
tional business, but took no part in the running of 
CBM UK - although he did work from their offices. 
Now he handles both that and hart of Ihe general 
managership of Commodore UK. 

Well, he seems to have the experience, but does 
he have the vision? We gave him the Third degree,, , 

CU: One question that wed tike 
to ask to dispel any rumours 
is how the changeover 
happened? it didthap- 
pen quite suddenly 
and there was 
no inkltng 
that 
changes 




were afoot, then suddenly we get a fax that 
Kelly has left and you Ve taken over. 

OP; Yes, you're absolutely nght that there was no 
inkling to anybody, including Commodore. I can toll 
you lhat pfiw to Ihe April ECTS show even Kelly 
didn't know. Basically he was approached at ECTS 
by an American software publisher, i ihink they do 
aboul $60-$70M a year of business..- 

CW is this GameTek? 

DP: ...yeah, which is not actually very large For an 
American software company but it is stilt a reason- 
able size and they are profitable. They have little or 
no presence in Europe. They sub-licensed Humans 
"o Mirage. 

What i understand: is lhat the opportunity thai 
Kelly has been given, which is a very good opportu- 
nity, is that he will be the Genera! Manager ol 
GameTek Europe. I believe the financial: potential 
would mean lhat il could be very rewarding to him. 

CU: So, now you're in charge, you've got a 
clean sheet, what are you going to do? 

DP One o( the things I'd like to point out is that 
Kelly worked tor me for a long time, and most ol the 
things he did were what we had been doing for 
some time so I have to say there'll be more ot the 
same. We have, I believe, a winning formula. 

CU' OK, tut there are a few problems. 
. Recently we have been getting a large 
amount of mail from people experiencing 
delays in the warranty service. Has this sit- 
uation cleared up? 

DP; Yes. We have spoken to Wang and told them 
that, due to the unprecedented business we have 
been doing, we don't believe they can give us the 
level of Support that we need. We are therefore 
looking to appoint a second, supplementary service 
to cater lor any overspill. 

CU: is this overspiff a result ot a larger 
than expected failure rate of the machine? 

DP: No, I'm guile happy thatlha machine is of suffi- 
cient quality. Both the A600 and A1 200 are surface 
mount lechnotogy - their failure level is extremely 
low and improving. I think it's lair to say that with the 
A1 200, as one would expect wifh new technology, 
lhere were some teething problems. But those were 
quickly spotted and sorted out. 

To be fair to Wang, when we signed that contract 
with them the A1200 didn't even e*isL We sold 
44,000 A1 300s in the Christmas period, a machine 
which they had no idea we were going to launch. 
We werent even sure we were going to launch il. 

CU: 1992 was one of the most successful 
years for the Amiga in terms ot sales, in 
fact it was the most successful, As thai is 
the case, why do you think that so many 
software companies are abandoning the

Amiga and concentrating on PC and con- 
sole titles f 

DP: Piracy. Absolutely unqualified piracy. The nel- 
work of piracy that exists - rt's not professional 

piracy, but amongsl the children at schorl - it's SO 
well established now. It's unthinkable- that you can 
launch a product like, for example, Zool. and not 
have a level of sates that bears any relationship id 
the number of machines out there. 

CU: But it's a vicious circle - less software 
available means the Amiga tooks less 
attractive in the marketplace. There is a 
rumour that you will be moving totally to 
CD software. 

DP: Well, there to no doubt that the future of this 
business does He <n CDs, and we are at the fore- 
front ol that. Whilst vwe have to acknowledge thai 
Ihe CDTV was not the greatest commercial venture 
rt certainly allowed us to ga<n a greai deal of exper- 
tise in the technology. We have a commitment that 
there will be a CO solution lor every Amiga platform 
we have - obviously that will include the A1 200, 

CU: Since at the moment it is impossible to 
buy a CD upgrade for any current Amiga, 
dan t you think that this is a priority if you 
want to cash in on any interest that the 
CDTV may have stirred up ? 

DP: We may weli have something pre-Christmas, 

but to be honest it is not an issue I have been chas- 
ing specrlicalty. Obviously I have more of an interest 
to chase it now, Having been at Commodore 10 
years (hough, I mean, l don't know if we invented 
the phrase 'vapourware" but wb are certainly as 
much a part o' that phitosophy as any other com- 
pany I just think it's wrong lo indicate 10 people that 
such and such a product is going to be available 
when it obviously isn't. 

CU. One thing which has been implied by 
Commodore UK in the past is that you 
don t have a great deal of say in what prod- 
ucts are actually developed. Do you think 
there is a tack of communication there? 

DP: Thai's an interesting point. I'd like to think that 

Ai a new boas al Comitwdor* wf i tnnouncad. a meeting of

|he relationship between sales & marketing world- 
wide and Commodore engineering is belter than n 
has ever been, and that's down to one man, the 
new head ot engineering Lew Eggebrschi. He is a 
rare specimen, a talented engineer who also has a 
great deal of commercial sense and he >s bringing 
lo market just what we need right now. Certainly in 
(he past rt has been the case that you've been 
called to a meeting and the engineers have said 
'Here guys, look what we've got for you', and you'd 
say 'Well, ihat's wonderful, but who asked for it?' 
Bui it doesn't happen thai way any more. * 

CU: There has been a lot of speculation 
about a CD console. Can you give us any • - 
more information on that? 

DP: As 1 said before I don't believe in saying things 
are going to appear before we have actual units 
here, but we are constantly developing new technol- 
ogy and we always market our products 
aggressively. If Ihere is an opportunity fhen we will 
do it. ! think we are leaders in that respect. 

CU: What about the higher end machines, 
the A4QQ9? Commodore hasn't really 
pushed product in the professional arena. 

DP: H depends on which country you are in. What 
you say is absolutely true in Ihe U.K., it's kind ol a 
cross that we bear. We happen to be the most suc- 
cessful ma/iulaclurerof home computers in the UK 
and people say lo us 'How can ! take your top end 
producls when you make games machines?'. 

The way I see Commodore is thai we own, 
undeniably, ihe home computer market. We also 
have these niche markets for the A4000 and 
A3OO0s, but ihe biggest of business is mostly IBM 
PC business and a bit of Apple Mac business. 

The PC market is rapidly going down the toilet - 
there is no money to be made, In the last three 
months 340 PC manufacturers in Taiwan have gone 
out of business. It's a market where everyone is 
selling the same spec machine and the only way to 
compete is on price. 

When we launched the Amiga ns graphics capa- 
bility was lar in advance of that on the PC I think it's 
lair lo say that we got fat and lazy, well, I certainly 
did, and we reached a stage where the PC got 
away from us wlh the SVGA mode. With the launch 



ol AGA we have bridged that gap and we are at the 
same level now We are currently testing the next 
step which will take us far ahead. 

In conjunction with that we are developing the 
RISC technology. Our plan is to have PCs which 
are fully DOS compatible through Windows and fuHy 
Amiga compatible in the hean ol the machine. 
These machines will be at a very competitive price 
point. I believe this will be very successful for us. 

CU: What about upgrades for the currant 
machines. Are you not concerned about 
Atari moving ahead with the DSP. 

DP: I don't think lhat there is a product ready (Or 
groups like musicians yet. I mean if you look al any 
of the Atan producls, it is of no use to anyone play- 
ing live. We will market a product when we have 
one that wtl work. Because of the size of that mar- 
ket it is likely to be a bundle There is no point 
weighing down a product with extra costs like a 
MIDI port when only 20 per cert! of users are going 
to do anything with them. Products like the Falcon 
don't worry us. We have a belter engine and we 
have good relationships with Roland and Yamaha. 

CU: Another popular question is are you 
planning a trade in deaf for the At 200? 

DP; I am trying lo work out the mechanics of one 
now, probably through the independent stores, and 
probably very soon. 

CU: Presumably the plan with the A600 is 
that you will continue to market that for 
some time to come. Is It destined to 
become the f>4 of the future? 

DP You've taken the words out of my mouth. We 
perceive that there is a need for an entry -level price. 
which shouldn't be more than £200. 1 think there is a 
potential for I hat product in that price range. 

CONCLUSION

ative boss at Commodore seems to have ihe

CITIZEN DEALER PLUS * 







y designing the Amiga with twth 
serial and parallel ports (sock- 
ets), Commodore have ensured 
I hat we can use virtually any printer on 
the market. But in order for the printer to 
be able to print anything, it needs to be 
given instructions in a specific format or 
language. In most cases, the Amiga 
speaks a slightly different dialect oMhe 
same language - some "words' are the 
same, but there are enough differences lo cause 
confusion. 

in order for the two lo communicate correctly, a 
translator is required. Enter the printer driver, a 




nmmiim 



small program that resides in the 
Devs; Printers directory of your 
Workbench disk or the Devs/'Pri titers 
drawer ol your hard drive. 

A number of driv&rs are on the Extras 
disk, which is supplied with your Amiga.- ; 
and these are likely to work with most 
printers. If not, you will find that additional 
drivers can be purchased from PD 
libraries. 

If you really can't find a suitable driver any- 
where, there are a number of programs available 
which you can use lo create your own driver from 
scratch. 






COPYCAT PRINTER 

ironically, the chances are you don't own a printer 
supported by the driver on your Workbench disk 
Those printers which sell in the largest volumes 
are not necessarily those which home users buy. 
After all business users still account for the largest 
sector of the printer market- The second factor 
concerns a feature called 'Emulation'. This is a 
printer's ability lo 'pretend' to be a different printer 
entirely,. SO lhal instructions thai were intended for 
one printer can be understood by any others which 
emulate it. In the business market, Laserjet and 
IBM Proprinler are two of the most popular printers 
to emulate. Your printer's manual will tell you 
which emulations it's capable of. 

To find out how to copy a driver onto your 
Workbench disk, see the box. Once that's done, 
you've got to tell your Amiga thai you want the new 
driver to be used. 

To do lhat, double-click the icon labelled Prefs 
on Ihe Workbench screen. When the Prefs window 
opens, double-click the Printer icon lo load the 
printer preferences program. This allows you to 
customise Ihe Amiga's output to suit the printer 
you're using. Although it has a different appear- 
ance under Workbench 1 .3 and 2,0, the options 
are identical. 

You must start by specifying whether you are 
connecting your printer lo the serial or parallel port 
The default is parallel and in most cases this will 
be correct, When you've done that, you need to 
indicate the printer driver that you will be using.

How printers work 



DOT MATRIX (IMPACT) 



A dot matrix is the least sophisticated in construc- 
tion of alt home printers. The prinl head contains 
nine, IS or 24 tiny iron pins arranged in a vertical 
strip. The more pins there are. the higher the reso- 
lution of the printer. There are also an equivalent 
number of tiny electromagnetic coils. As the print 
head passes over the paper,, electricity is passed

BUBBLE JET 

For tine last couple of years bubble jet printers 
have been wowing computer users with their 
superb quality to price ratk). For less than a third of 
ihe price of the cheapest laser, bubble jet printers 
such as the Canon BJ1Q, Star SJ48 and the Kodak 
Dice- nix can produce near-laser output at a resolu- 
tion of up to 360 dpi, *. 



SIMPLIFIED DIAGRAM OF A 
BUBBLE JET CARTRIDGE 



pii- tmi— 







Bps'O* 



The mosl important part of modern bubble jet 
printers is the replace ment ink cartridge. Unlike 
other lypes ol printer in which the print heads and 
ink (or toner) are two separate entities, Ihe two 
have been integrated into a single disposable unit 
where the BJs are corcerned- 

A bubble jet cartridge consists ol an ink reser- 
voir containing a piece of sponge which is there lo 
ensure a consistent and din-free ink supply lo the 
heads. 

The heads are also built into the cartridge and 
these are essentially nothing more than straightfor- 
ward ho(k>w lubes with heaters attached to them. 
Initially ink is fed lo heads by capillary action 
alone, but once priming has begun this IS assisted 
by suction. 

If you look at the Six Step diagram ol a bubble 
jet nozzle, pictured above right* you'll see how a 
droplet of ink is transferred to the paper. 

1 . The nozzle is lull ol ink and waiting to go. 



Enlarged View ol a Bubble Jet No/zle Showing 1h 
Stages Involved in Printing a Single Dot 




• ' 


' _ t- 


. V . 


_> • 


■. : _, L * 


) ''..—!* 



2. The piezo electric heater warms up and air 
bubbles form in the ink above it 

3*4. Because the capillary teed tubes are SO 
small, the ink cannot flow back into them quickly 
enough, so as the air bubbles expand, ink is forced 
out of the open end of ihe nozzle. 

5. As the heater is lumed off, Ihe ink rapidly 
flows back into Ihe nozzle. This contraction severs 
the droplet which had previously been forced out 
of the nozzle^ firing it directly at the nearby sheet of 
paper, 

6. The element is cold, the quick drying ink is 
on ihe paper, and the nozzle is refilled by a combi- 
nation of vacuum and capillary action.

LASER 

Of all Ihe primers suitable for home use that are 
available on Ihe market today, a laser printer is 
undoubtedly the mosl complex in te design and 
construction. 

At ils heart lies a mirror- surfaced photosensitive 
metal drum upon which the ink is placed, and 
which in turn transfers the ink onto Ihe paper. 

in order lor the drum to hold the Ink it has to be 
given a negative charge of about -&00 volts. This is 
done via the primary corona wire (3). When the 
entire surface of the drum is negatively charged, a 
laser beam (4) is used lo selectively remove the 
charge from parts of the drum. 

it does this in such a way as to create a ion- 
changed area which is a mirrored template ol Ihe 
image that you want to eventually appear - the 
areas which are to hold the ink have no charge,

The relating drum passes by the developing 
station (5) Inside the developing station , toner 
powder has also been negatively charged and 
placed in suspension so that it forms a cloud of 
micro -fine powder. The parts of the drum lhal still 
hold a negative charge, repel the toner powder, 
whereas the powder is attracted to the parte thai 
have no charge 

The drum continues to rolale until it reaches a 
point where il passes the paper on which the 
image is to be printed. On the other side of the 
paper is the Transfer corona wire (6). This emits 
positive ions giving She paper a very strong positive 
charge. This charge is so strong that it literally rips 
Ihe negatively charged toner particles from the sur- 
face of the drum, and onto the paper 

The paper moves beyond the drum ar>d its 
charge is removed lo slop the tor>er from being 
attracted lo areas other than those which are sup- 
posed to contain ink. 

GRAVITY 

From now on, the ink powder is literally held on the 
paper by gravity until the paper reaches the (using 
rollers (7). The fusing rollers heat the paper as it 
passes and because Hie toner is a compound 
made of black plastic and iron, it melts onto the 
surface of the paper, where it will then remain per- 
manently. 

The next lime that a page rs printed, a cleaning 
blade (1) first scrapes any remaining ink Iron the 
surface of Ihe drum. Erase lamps {2} then dis- 
charge any remaining potential that the drum may 
hold, thus ensuring that its surface is dean and 
holds no charge m readiness for the cycle lo begin 
again. 

If many copies of The same page are printed, 
the drum does not go through this cleaning cycle

SERIAL VS PARALLEL - 
WHAT'S THE DIFFERENCE? 

You may have noticed that there are both serial 
and parallel ports on the back of your Amiga. If you 
know much about pnniers, you may be aware that 
they also come equipped with a serial or parallel 
interface, or even both. So what's the deal? Why 
nave two different types of interface at all? 

To understand lhal, we have to first look at 
what exactly is transmitted down ihe line every 
time you (ell your printer to print. A printer is com- 
pletely dependant upon control (or escape) codes 
and character or pixel data. The control codes are 
instructions which tell the printer how lo interpret 
and output the data. 

Bolh control codes and character/pixel data are 
nothing more than streams of numbers, and these 
numbers are sent to Ihe printer as binary digits _ 
ones and zeros. Eight binary digits {or bits as 
Ihey're known) are required to send a single char- 
acter of information to the printer. 

PARALLEL LINES 

As its name suggests, a parallel interface allows 

you to send a number of bits Of information at a 
time (in parallel). In fact, a parallel cable can han- 
dle eights bits (one byte) al a lime. A serial 



T v :hra# tsksur {.Etrtporewi itUH 
■thai rr-K.ii y jjijj'ier ■ ; 



Hk) 



Bc:auie a printer doesn t nave an infi- 
nite number at cHHawrat eolourwj ir>k», it 
h» In iim U l»i» varying intensities ol 
co lour by using a variety ol drther pat- 
terns. Itve pure rolau' ia in Ihe mlddta 
o1 lite cctaur ilrip. To make the colour 
lighter a piin|»r simply prints ln# dots 
ot coloured ink further apart m thai Ihe 
while paper dilutes the colour To- cr*a(t 
darKer colour increasingly greater 
amount* Ot black Ink are added la the 
origin*! col«J' ■ 

interface is only capable ol han- 
dling one bit at a time, so as you 

can imagine, transmission of 
information via this interface is 
considerably slower. So why 
bother about serial transmission 
at all whan parallel is so much 
faster?, 

The reason is one of data 
integtety The parallel interface 
uses standard TTL volt levels of 
- and 46 volts, whereas fjhe 
serial port uses a range of +1 £ lo -12 volts. 
Because the differential between the two states is 
so great with serial transmission, it s far easier to 
delect and interpret Ihe signal, and lar less likely 
that any interference (known as line noise) will dis- 
tort the results The signal will not dissipate quite 
as quickly through losses in the wire. This makes 
serial transmission sdaal for printer link-ups over 
longer distances (anything above three metres), or 
in circumstances where there is likely lo be a high 
amount of line noise 

In the interests of accuracy, I should just men- 
tion that some serial devices oniy generate a 
voltage of */-& volts, giving a voltage differentia! of 
to volts which is stilt double that of parallel trans- 
mission. 

Thanks to Dave Butler and Adrian Telford of 
GEC Sensors lor their help with this section.

and basic escape and control code sequences used by your

This is really a hybrid machine. Called a thermal 
transfer printer, it transfers material from a ribbon 
to the page using a heated print head. The heat 

means thai although The pins are very much like 
those found on a normal dPl matrix printer, the 
area of material on the ribbon affected is slightly 
greater than the pin area. This eliminates the

spaces between dots which are normally visible on 
a dot matrix and helps to smooth the edges of the_ 
pixels. 

The advantage of such a system as opposed lo

paper, is that the ribbons are a damned sight 
cheaper than the paper used to be. Even so, at 
best a single £3 50 ribbon only just manages half a

EPSON SQ870 

The Epson SQB70 is a direct rival lor the Citizen 

Projet. and ii wins hands down on every possible 
count, except price. It costs a massive £659 
excluding VAT - more expensive than many laser 
printers I 

The mosl impressive thing about this printer 
must be its print speed. In draft mode, it posi lively 
sears the page with a blistering 550 characters per 
second That's Six and a quarter pages per 
minule; most cheaper lasers aren't that fasti 

When we tested Ihis printer, it took us a measly 
one minute and 28 seconds to print a lull screen 
from Deluxe Paint a a resolution ol 360x360dpi. If 
you haven'! got I he message yet T this is a seri- 
ously las! printer. 

It comes suppited with a tractor teed for contin- 
uous stationery, but unfortunately doesn't include 
an automatic sheel feeder as standard, allhough 
you can add one later if ypu like. I felt that this was 
quite a serious ommission considenng the 
ornter's price. 

With a RAM bulferol 128k as standard, you 
can download most screens to it in a single hit, 
and even DTP pages will be much faster to output. 

It comes with a generous selection of nine 
typefaces and these can be further upgraded as 
can Ihe memory. The ink cartridges are reason 
ably pr iced at £23 lor a cartridge with a six million 
character lifespan. 

Obviously this printer is not aimed a! Ihe home 
user It would seem to be ideally suited to people 
whose primary requirement is the fast, quiet print 
inc. of lots ol text. 

Available Irom; Epson UK, Campus 1 00, 
Mary lands Avenue, Heme! Hempstead, 
Herts, HP2 7EZ, Tel: 0442 G1144. {^f^jk 

Price: ES59 (ex VAT). * mjfP

BUYER'S GUIDE CU AMIGA 



The OL400E is an LEO printer, a close relative to 
fie laser priiler. The main advantage ol LED 
pruning is thai it doesn't emrt ozone depleting 
gases as laser printing does. 

The OL400E produces no such harmful sub- 
stances, and consequently the entire mechanism 
© considerably smaller than that of its fretler- 
known relatives. 

Unlike the other devices reviewed here, the 
OL400E is a page printer - that means that it 
prints a whole page rather than a line at a tine, It 
can manage four pages per minute, which is 
equivalent lo about 352 characters per second, 
Even a full Deluxe Paint screen only look one 
minute and six seconds to print Irom start to finish, 
and duplicates ol the same screen only took 1 5 
seconds each. 

The 0L4QD only comes with 51 2K which isn't 
very much lor a laser-type printer. However; it 
uses a special Reduced Instruction Set Chip 
(RISC') to enhance the way that graphics are han- 
dled. This means that, unlike other page printers, 
51 2K is perfectly adequate tor a ful' A4 page ot 
graphics. 

Unusually for this sort of pnnter. it only comes 
with four typelaces, although these are very 
attractrve when printed, 

Overall, the OL4QCE produces crisp, visually 
pleasing print. It's by far the cheapest pnnter to 
run with replacement toner cartridges only costing 
£17.99, Considering Ihe fact thai they last for 
about 1 500-2000 pages {about 7,920,000- 
10,560.000 characters), i| really is a great , 
machine for Ihe professional on a budget. 
Available from: Okl Systems (UK) Ltd, 
550 Dundee Road, Slough, Berks, SLi 
4JY. Tel : 0753 SI 981 9. Price: £499,

QUALITY TIME 

animation 

I was beginning to think Eric Schwartz had given 
up on his PD cartoons This is the first he's 
released for ages, and is another essential for all 
animation lans. You'll need 4Mb to run it, and 
unless you fancy spending your whole day swap- 
ping disks, you'll also- need a second dish drive 10 
load it, It's a simple plot, basically Flip (tie Frog try- 
ing to have his wicked way with Amy Ihe Squirrel, 
hampered by constant interruptions from the door- 
bell. How Erie manages to make a squirrel look 
sexy. I don't know. The animation is brilliant as 
always, and there's plenty of speech and sound 
effects. II you've got the necessary hardware, get a 
copy without delay. 

Available from: 17 Bit, 1st Floor Offices, 2/8 
Market Street, Wakefield, WF1 1DH. Tel:

(Including PIP)

per bars and logos, with some run-of-the-mill backing

PLANET GROOVE 

demo 

Attention all 1200 owners I This is the first demo on 
the scene lo make use ol the new AGA 256-colour 
mode, although unfortunately it doesn't manage to 
do anything particularly stunning with it. 

What you get on this disk are some nice 
exploding firework and dot sine -scroll effects on a 
256-colour graduated backdrop To be honest, 
none of the effects would look out of place on a 
standard A50C demo. The graduated backdrops 
are probably bitmaps, but they may as well be cop- 
per lists. Slil I, the swirly colours are nice, and it's 
good to see someone making an effort There 
must be potential for some mind-warping demos 
with the AGA chips, but this one certainly isn't 
going lo cause a commotion.

BALLOONACY 



Do you remember that game where you fly across a skyline of office 
blocks, trying to bomb them all before your plane loses altitude arid 
crashes? Well this is the same game, only this time you're in a bal- 
loon. The trouble is thai it's far too easy. As 11 it weren't simple 
enough, you're given sandbags, which can be jettisoned lo give you 
more time. It's a pleasant enough diversion, but a retarded chimp 
wearing a blindfold wouldn't have any difficulty pressing the button at 
the right lime. Suitable for three year-olds who can't fathom out PacMan. 
Available from : Cynostic PD, 85 Wyken Croft Road, Coventry, CV2 3AD. Tel: 0203 
682084. Disk no. G0O82. Price: £2.00 (including P&P) 




MIRROR 



music 

At first this looks like it's going to be a full-blown demo. The intro 
effect is clever, bul it can hardly be described as exciting. From 
then on, it's a fairly simple two-disk music demo with a selection of 
nine lories. All of them are very neatly sampled and produced, but 
they lend to be uneventful in places. Rather than tunes that you'd 
sit down and listen to. they're more appropriate as background 
music, while you're doing the ironing or something else that's 
rather tedious. If electric pianos, organs and slapped basses 
sound like your cup of tea, you'll appreciate this tot. The front end 
is nicely done, but could do with some jiggling VU meters, wibbly 
graphic effects, or at least something olher than the scrolltext. 
Another one for the demo lune collectors. 
Available from: Cynostic PD, 85 Wyken Croft Road, Coventry, 
CV2 3 AD. Tel: 0203 5S2084. Disk no. 50056 a+b. 
Price: £200 (including P&P). 




LIBERATOR 



It's twe, you can write a hopeless shoot 'em up 
without the aid of Shoot Em Up Construction Kil\ 
Just to prove it, here's Liberator. Featuring some of 
the most uninspired aliens ever, (his game puts 
you in control of a ship flying up a vertically 
scrolling background. Your pea-shooter cannon is 
barely enough lo destroy (tie aliens, and even 
when you do kill them, they absolutely refuse to 
explode I As if this wasn't bad enough, you only get 
one life. Take a look if you want lo know how not to 
write a shoot "em up.

Tony Morgan continues his look at all things PD with a 
comprehensive round-up of all the latest utilities. 

DISABLE CDTV 



utility 

If you've got a CDTV, you may be well feeing 
father cheated. Although it's quoted as hav- 
ing 1Mb of RAM, the system takes aboui 50K 
ol that to help it operate the CD drive effi- 
ciently. You might think that 50K from tQOOK 
isn't oping to make much difference, but it 
you're running a program thai uses every last 
byte of a 1 Mb machine, it can make all the 
Htonncs. 

Applications software is usually config- 
urable m a number of forms, which can often 
avoid incompatibility problems, but quite a 
lew games have fallen foul of the memory 
loss. In this very issue, we lest a hardware 
solution to the problem (page 88), but at £2.00, 
the software allemative does sound tempting. 

The utility itsell comes on an autobooting disk, 
but the idea is that you add it to your startup 
sequence. It cures the memory shortage problem 
it you're using AmigaDOS disks, but doesn't sur- 
vive a reset, so it's no use with most games. This 
is a bit ol a shame, as its games that are worst

One of the most striking new features on ' 
Workbench 3.0 is the one that lets you 
put a multi-colour backdrop On your 
Workbench window. The trouble is, rl 
your best attempts with DP&nt resemble 
the scnbblirvgs of a three year old, it's still 
not going to be enough to impress your 
mates What you need to make a reat 
impression is a collection ol stylishly 
drawn oven ready backdrops, which 
could be slipped in behind your icons , 
whenever you wanted- 

Well that's handy, because thaf s 
exactly what we've got here. Workbench 
3.0 Screens is a. disk of 13 IFF images 
designed just for that purpose. Most are 
shaded textures, the kind ol thing you'd 
see behind the football results Qh the 
news. A few others are mare like letter- 
heads, Most ol them work well on an 
uncluttered 1 Workbench, although some 
can make things look rather loo busy, rl 
you've got a lot of icons and windows on 
the go. 

Some of the more subtle examples 
could double as effective sources tor art 
packages and 30 texture- mapping. Get 
a copy and kiss goodbye to that tedious 
grey Workbench.

SUPER SOUND 3.2 

sound utilities 

I don't know about you, bul I can never have too 
many sample editors OctaMED handles 95 per 
cent of my sampling workload, but there's no beat- 
ing AMAS Its brilliant reverb, or AudioMaster IVs 
time stretching. The trouble is, just lhal tno ol pro- 
grams could set you back a fair tew quid, and il 
you're only going to use one or two features from 
each, it can seem even more expensive. The solu- 
tion? Super Sound ot course. 

Lesser versions of Super Sound have been 
around for a while, but with the new 3.2 release, it's 
ready to take on anything the commercial sector 
can offer. The sample quality is on a par with the 
competition, art rates of up lo 24kHz in either mono 
or stereo, but where it really excels is in the editing. 

Apart from all the Usual curling and pasting 
options, there are two menus packed with special 
effects. Almost all of them are variable, so you can 
tailor them to suit your sounds. 

The sound-warping options include bass boost, 
treble boost, unfilter, smooth wave, tremolo, echo, 
reverse echo, reverb, reverse reverb, lowpass filter, 
bighpass filter, volume, metallic, treble waah, fade 
in and out, lilier waah. unfilter waah, mix, reverse. 
octave up and down, phaser, distort and flange 
The waah effects are a first, as are the reverse 
reverts and echoes. 

There's still some work to be done on me reverb 
routine, but the flanger and phaser are bolh excel- 
lent. The only problem I could find, is that when 
playing samples with a loop, the loop jumps back 
slightly early. 

The fun doesn't stop there either. The Realtime 
Effects Generator included on the disk is just as 
impressive, giving you the ability to add delays, 
echoes, phases and various types of distortion to 
any incoming sound, with surprisingly clean results- 
A very basic sequencer is on the disk too. 

KBE Software publish a professional looking 
manual, with annotated screenshots, hotkey lists 
and tutorials, which is available for £7 Anyone 
who's seriously into sampling would be a complete 
and utter fool to pass up the superb quality offered 
by Super Sound 3.2. This could be your last 
chance to get il on ire cheap - it's bound to cross 
Over to the commercial market within an upgrade or 
two, so get your copy now! 
Available from: Syslec PD, 2 Ridge Road, 
Letchworth, Herts, SG6 1PN, Tel: 0462 483604. 
Disk no. CLU06. Price: E5-25 (including 
P&P). Compatibility: All Amlgas. 
Memory: 1 Mb.

It you're a regular user of bulletin boards or PD 
software, you'd be familiar with ANSI graphics, 
even if you dKln't realise it. You know those funny 
intra screens you gel when you log on, or boot up 
a PD dish (the or>es that look like they've been 
designed on a Spectrum)? Well they're made up of 
ANSI graphics. Instead ot being stored as mem- 
ory-hungry IFF bitmaps, they're made from far 
more efficient keyboard characters. 

The rise in popularity ot ANSI graphics is curi- 
ous. While most are raving over 24-bit ray-tracings 
others are getting excited Over backslashes and 
asterisks. 

ANSI Gallery is primarily a showcase of various 
ANSI tides and animations, but it also allows you to 
create your own, thanks to the ed«lor that's 
included on the disk (Prism 1 ,5). If you've been 
using a text- editor until now, this will seem too 
good lo be true! It's a very simple design, but 
works extremely well. You position the cursor on 
the screen with (tie mouse, and then enter the 
character from the keyboard. New colours can be 
selected from the palette at the bottom of me 
screen. Wilhin a few minutes you can knock up an 
Intro for your next demo or bulletin board, and all St 
costs in memory is a few kilobytes. 

Whether you've got [he editor or not, give it a 
go if you're info ANSI graphics, as mere are plenty 
of examples of still and animated screens on the 
disk that should inspire your own creations 
Available from' PD Soft, 1 Bryant Avenue, 
Southend on Sea Essex, SS1 2VD, Tel: 0702 
612259 466933- Disk no. V1O07. Price: 
£2.00. Compatibility: All Amigas. 
Memory: 51 2K.

CHORD TUTOR 

muii< th«ory 

I don't know about you, but I never fancied piano 
lessons (and if you ever met my old music teacher 
you'd see why). Instead, I've got by with a self- 
taught repertoire consisting ol the bassline from 
Kilter, the opening bars of St&pioe and Son, and 
three chords from the Twin Peaks theme - it's 
amazing how far you Can blulf your way with such 
a palhelic arsenal ol musical prowess. 

On the other hand, there are times when you 
might wanl lo really push the boat out and 
learn a whole new set Ot Chords, in which 
case Chord Tutor could be just the thing 
you're after. 

There's plenty of scope for a chord 
tutor program, and at first this looks like it 
could be quite helpful. Most of the screen 
is taken up by a large two-octave key- 
board. By clicking on the keys wilh the 
pointer, you can play single notes, Chords 
can be selected from the menus, and as 
they're played, the notes are highlighted 
with dots. Once you think you've learnt 
most ol Ihe chords, you can pul the pro- 
gram into lest mode This asks yod to 
input a chord, and you then have to click 
the pointer on the right notes. The trouble 
■is, you can only click on one nole at a 
time, so you can't actually play the chord! 



Soundtracker- style keyboard input would have 
helped, and MIDI support would have been a very 
simple but extremely effective addition, The chorda 
and notes are played on one of Ihree grating synih 
sounds, not piano samples as you might have 
expected, There's plenty of potential in Chord 
Tutor, bul for the time being I think I'll slick to 
Steptoe and Son. 

Available from: Syslec PD, 2 Ridge Road, 
Lelchworth, Herts, SG6 1PN. Tel: 0462 4&3604. 
Disk no, CLE 25. Price: £3.50. Memory: 
S12K. Compatibility: All Amigas 




SPEED UTILITIES VI .0 

utility coin pi lotion 

One of the more user-friendly utility compilations. Speed Utilities has a generous offering ol handy pro- 
grams, all accessed from a neat pull-down menu system. The featured utilities are DCopy, Super Duper, 
tmptoder V4.0, PP Patches Key Map Editor, Snoop, Systnfo. Tsxtra, Virus Checker, Zap, Boo/copy, 
Fixdtsk, Headcieart and last Hope, along with Workbench, Clock, Shell and 1.3 Preferences. 

There's nol enough room here tor a correlate description of each, hut here's a quick rundown. D-Copy 
is probably the best PD copier available, with a good nibble mode. Super Duperls a simple WB 2+ 
AmigaDOS copier and disk formatter. Imptodgr compresses execylable files. Keymap Editor lets you alter 
your keymap via a friendly interface, Snoopdds keeps you updated on the comings and goings ol files 
under Workbench, and Sysinfo 3.0 1 will tell you everything you want to know about your Amiga [and 
more!). Texfra 2-0 is a text editor. Virus Checker is up lo version 6,20, and Zap is a binary file editor. 
Bootcopy copies bootblocks, Fixdtsk is a more reliable- Diskdoctor replacement. Last Hope attempts to 
recover deleted files, and Head Clean is for use with head-cleaning disks. 

Speed Utilities is definitely one of the best compilations available, and deserves a place in anyone's 
disk collection. 

Available from: PD Soft, 1 Bryant Avenue, Southend- on -Sea, Essex, SS1 2YD. Tel: 0702 
&1 2259 466933- Disk no. V1006. Price: E2.Q0: CompatibiNly: All Amigas. Memory; 51 2K.

If you've ever 
bought a 
record, 
chances are 
the shop had a 
TV or video 
displaying the latest hits. 
Would you be shocked to 
learn that it's probably 
controlled by an Amiga? 
Jon Sloan goes behind 
the scenes to speak to 
the man responsible.

PROFILES 




Many of us have heard the term 'multime- 
dia' bandied around. But how many can 
claim to truly know what it means and 
how lo use it to ils best erf eel? One man can. Chris 
Fulton is the brains behind a simple mutlimedia 
player itiat has nened him and his company, 
Future Software Systems, a healthy client list of 
corporate names that include Virgin, Our Pridfe, 
and Granada Pic 

Future was termed in 1 965 to provide consult- 
ing and bespoke systems development to new ' ■ 
computer graphic and video users. The Managing 
Director and only full-time staff member, Chris 
Fulton, first got involved in computers way back in 
1Q77 when he attended a computer course After 
successfully completing thai he embarked on a 
freelance career as a software engineer/systems 
analyst working on a vast range of platforms, from 
super computers, such as the CDC 6000/600, to- 
standard desktop machines, like the PC. 

EARLY DAYS 

'I set up Future mainly as a consultancy for high- 
end systems users, a bit like Rent A Scientist - il 
you've got a problem we'll find a solution.' Almost 
immediately Chris recognised the potential of com- 
puter graphics and how important they ware 
becoming to his clients. 'Atihe same time a friend 
called Tony Prince, who was a OJ for Radio 
Caroline, was putting on various shows al the 
Hippodrome and wanted some graphics for them. I 
helped him out and it went down a treat- From 
there I started to do more and more work lor the 
entertainment industry and. when l saw what was 
happening in the professional retail market, I 
decided lo write a multimedia player aimed atlhaj.' 
This program, later called Limettte Junior, is a 
video typewriter and special effects generator for 
use in pubs, cates and small nightclubs. With 
expenence at IBM behind him Chris wrote it on a 
PC and it was lirst used in a project al the Albert 
Hall. However, he was less than happy about the 
results: It cost us an arm and a leg to get the IBM 
graphics into a suitable video signal to project onto 
a video wall, I wasn't that impressed by the quality 
of graphics available and couldnl see anyone pro- 



out now singles

ducing the hardware to handle them.' Fortunately, 
the A500 appeared on the market soon alter and 
when Chris looked at its specs he decided (hat H 
was ideal for his purposes. He decided lo port his 
program across onto it and the firsi systems were 
supplied to chenls in November 1987. 

MOVING ON 

It was updated a year later to include a control 1 for 
genlocks, remote update and triggering capabilities 
and, more importantly, external device suppod via 
the serial port. This meant that Limettte 
Professional, as it became, could control VCFI&, 
Lighting Controllers, Video Wall electronics and 
other vital mutlimedia machines, Chris could sell 
this solution not only lo small pubs and the like but 
lo large concert venues and chain store retailors. 

Future's latest project is a software solution for 
multimedia applications, called Limettte Montage. 
It's a hands oft' multimedia player. Its main use is 
tor retailers and other users who need bright, witty 
'ways ol getting across key info without the hassle 
of inputting text. Basically the screen images can 
be made up of pictures, text, pictograms, arid 
audio all mixed together using Montage's built in 
real time timers and genlock control facilities. 
However, as a hands off' device it hasn't got a true 
customer front end at the moment, So Chns plans 
to look at developing one so that it can be placed 
in hotels and the like where there's a neod for fairly 
frequent text editing by the user. 

He s not modest about his success, and rightly 
so considering the quality of clients that he s 
served- Well, let's hope that he can persuade more 
large corporations that the Amiga truly is an excep- 
tional business machine. Chris can be contacted 
on 0628 2431 8. ®

BOOK REVIEWS 




Nick Veitch delves into the two latest 
tomes from Addison-Wesley. 



STRUCTURE 5 AND STRATEGIES FOR COMPLEX 
PROBLEM SOLVING (2ND EDITION) 

LUGER STUBBUFIELD 

There are a great many books which cover, or at least attempt to cover, the 
growing field ol artificial intelligence. Many of them are either loo vague or too 
difficult for the layman to break into. This book is one of the rare examples 
which isn't either. 

The introduction is a bit bnef , giving a concise bin very useful background 
to the science and history ot thinking machines. After this the reader is hurled 
straight into the calculus of logic. A predicate calculus lor emulalirxj thought is 
derived and tested, with all the logical functions defined and proved Irom first 
principles- 

The or© important aspect of this book is that it deals with the logic of prob- 
lem solving lirsl, belore looking at ways ot representing this in computer code. 
This means that the calculus evolved is just as relevant to problem solving in 
the human brain as it is to the computer. The only Trouble is thai you have to 
be able to understand it. 

This book is not for beginners. A background m mathematics and a reason- 
able programming knowledge would be very useful for anyone attempting to 
decipher the processes behind the algorithms discussed in the text. A thor- 
ough understanding of Boolean algebra is definitely a bonus when working 
through the first half of the book. 

A detailed analysis of the mechanics behind heuristics, space searches, 
knowledge bases and genetic algorithms fill the 720 pages, accompanied by 
diagrams of varying degrees of usefulness. The only area Of Al which has 
been neglected is fuzzy logic, which scarcely gets a mention. This is a bit ol a 
shame as it is one area Of Al which is becoming quite popular again, and 
could certainly do with some rigorous explaining on the programming side. 
However, a rather interesting section on Neural networks makes up for this. 
Most of the algorithms are explained in terms of Prolog and Lisp, two lan- 
guages specifically designed for Al applications. A book at this level is not 
going to bother about translating the listings into BASIC, but the code is really 
more to do with techniques than specific examples. If you have a version ot 
Prolog or Lisp lor the Amiga. I strongly recommend that you buy this book. 

It is a bit on the 
expensive side, but con- 
sidering the depth and 
scope of the book it is 
very good value. 
Because of its complex- 
ity though, il is difficult 
to recommend this book 
to anyone who is not ■ 
student In this field 

It does, however, 
achieve what rt sets out 
to do though - examine 
the processes by which 
decisions are made and 
construct workable com- 
puter algorithms to 
perform Ihe task 
ISBN 0-JJG53-4780-1 
720 pa g*5, £24.95. 
Published by Addison- 
Wesley, 

Finchampstead Rd. 
Wokingham, Berks., 
R011 2N2. 
Tel: 0734 7*1000 





POSTSCRIPT BY EXAMPLE 

MCGlLTON/CAMPflONE 

A lot ol people do not 
realty understand the 
principles behind the 
Adobe Postscript stan- 
dard. Hisnotiusta 
different protocol for 
printing images, it is a 
different technique for 
storing and transport- 
ing them too. 

Postscript is not 
realty a hie format 
(although there are file 
formats associated 
with il) but is more like 
a programming lan- 
guage in its own right. 
As with structured 
the images 10 be 
pnnted are defined not 
by a bitmap of data, 
but by mathematically 
defined shapes. II is a 
page description lan- 
guage, because the 
data is not a physical 
image in itself, but 

rather the instructions lor how to generate that image. Tfws makes the infor- 
matJon more compact and more resilient to resolution changes, which in 
turn makes it very useful lor any task which has a printer in it somewhere. 

There are a number of books which deal with the formal of the language 
and ihe structure ol its commands. This is no! one of those books, 

Postscript By Example is more like a language tutorial, which explains 
how algorithms and routines can be constructed in this language This may 
seem a bit daft to some people - after all. who wants to program a printer? 

Well, actually, you could have a point there. But writing little pieces of 
Code to pnni out graduated spheres is very educational. It's a bit like the 
Amenean Apollo missions. The real end result was not a few nice 
Hasseloiad pictures oj the earth from a distance and people saluting the flag 
the rea< achievement "was Teflon, microprocessors and Pot Noodles (weiL 
not all inventions end up being beneficial}. So it is with this book. At the end 
you may be able to do some neat party tricks, but the important thing is thai 
along the way you wilt have tearpt a great deal about how the postscript 
system works. 

The knowledge gained can give you a gteaier unders'anding of how the 
whole system works, from the origination software to the Postscript inter- 
preters on printers, it could also be a useful programming technique tor Ihe 
future, The NeXt platform uses Postscnpt as a screen-display driver and 
other systems may follow, so anything you team could be of great advan- 
tage Eater on. 

Primarily though, this book is for the kind of people who have to com- 
pletely understand something before they feel happy using it. is your 
bedroom or workshop filled with little bits of clocks, a tew PCBs Ifom long- 
dead Acorn Atoms and enough leftover parts from knackered TV sets to 
keep a repair shop going for over a year? Then this is the book for you. 

The pace of (earning is well balanced and you will be writing your 
Postscript program in less than 10 minutes. Advanced topics such as pat- 
terned fills, halftones, clipping paths and fonts are covered, but each 
discovery is preceeded by a well structured introduction to all the compo- 
nents necessary, so nothing comes as too much of a surprise, Even if 
everything goes wrong there is a section on writing your own error-handler 
to work out what the problem is. When you have finished this book you will 
understand Encapsulated Postscript and be able 10 write your own 
Postscripl handling software, as well as being able to write in pure 
Postscripl 

The more pedestrian aspects of fonts are explained, including the 
makeup Of composite fonts (invaluable in Asian and Oriental countries) and 
explanations of some of Ihe different formats. There are better books for 
explaining (he font formats, but this is sufficient tor anyone who isn't going to 
try and hard-code one themselves. 

Actua-ly, this book is not the deflnilrve reference book for Postscript, but 
it is an excellent introduction - in fact, you may never need another 
Postscripl manual. 
ISBN 0-20163228-4 

520 pages. £24.95. Published by Addison- Wesley, Hnchampstead Rd, 
Wokingham, Berks., RG11 2NZ. Tel: 0734 794000

13909®

Activating Show will display the currently selected 
font and size: the font can be emboldened, itali- 
cised or underlined by activating (he appropriately 
labelled buttons. There is an upbeat side to font 
loading, and that's ColorText fonts, These are spe- 
cially designed typefaces which include colour 
information in their definition, They are usually 
highly stylised - for instance wooden blocks, 
stained glass and chrome fonts are available - and 
demand their own palette changes to work, Before

This month we take a 
look at some of 
Workbench's lesser 
known features. 



The interesting thing about the 
Amiga's Workbench is that there 
is a great deal of it which a large 
number of users have no idea exists 
There is a whole sub-strata of files 
and directories which are invisible from 
the standard WIMP syslem, These are 
the files thai control things like the 
floppy drives, the Ram Disk, the serial 
port, the commands which are automat- 
ically executed on start-up and various other 
control and preference tiles. 





AOJ 

CLI is short for command line environment This is 
a way of using the computer to perform tasks with- 
out using the mouse and all those icons and oiher 

IF THI SHELL SUITS**. 

She version I .3 of Workbench, the CU has hod on ohef ego colled the 'Shell', mis is the inter- 
face most often used. There are several ooWrioos of the shell over the dd CU, ml the least of 
whidi is ihol it has u mercery. 

If you ore typing in the sbefl you am real any previous tine vou hove entered by pressing 
the turarup key {the arrow panting up}. This is extremely useful when trying to repeat multiple 
Me operations, Mother irnpartsiTl fJfR&rerxie to the sbeH ts thai ytnj <&ri etlif the line ds you eftter 
it, using both delete keys and me cursor left end right keys. There is nothing more frustrating 
than getting to the end of a long pathname and avowing fhol you've misspelled the device 
nome. . . espeoolty if you ihfin have Eo type the whole line again. 

Another useful feature is the 'olios' command, another labour saving device. This allows you 
to define your own shorttul oommonds. The format is simple - the First parameter is the shortcut, 
the second is the full command. For example: 

cms k to hwiHyperiKMik 
would mean thai every time you use the command V in the Shell, you wi end up in o oratory 
died 'rtyperbook' on tt device called 'horaV. 



girlie things. People graduating from other 
machines may have experienced some- 
thing like the CLI before. 

At the most basic level, the CLI is just 
an interpreter which accepts single com- 
mands as inpul and executes ihem when 
the return key is pressed. 

This is rather similar to the way in which 
interpreted languages work in their direct 
mode. So really, to use the CLI all you 
need to Know is what the commands are add how 
exactly to use them. 

On the Amiga, almost all of ihe CLI commands 
are actually stored as executable files on the 
Workbench disk. They live in a special directory on 
the disk simply called "c". Whenever you enter a 
command, the operating syslem Hrsl checks if the 
command is resident (explained later), then the 

current directory and 
finally the 'C directory 
on the startup disk. 
It is important to 
take this on board, 
because if there are 
two executable files 
wi|h the same name 
(for example, different 
versions ol the same 
command), then the 
one that is actually 
execuled is the one 
that is found first. Be 
careful. 

It is possible to 
extend this search to 
other direclones by 
using Ihe "path' com- 




a 



Add a query 10 your command and a tut or indecipherable 
options can be yours 

mend- This is an easy command, for which ihe 
syntax is simply 

Path narnel name2 add 

where 'namel ' and 'name2' are Ihe names of Ihe 
palhs you wish to search. These are lull path- 
names, including the device name. An example 
might be: 

Path Extras [commands new 
commands Ram commands add 

which would add the two named directories to the 
search. This is the reason why you are always 
asked to replace the Workbench disk when you 
are executing a command through the CLI, 

because the C directory that is contained on your 
startup-disk is automatically placed at the top ol 
the list.

TAKING RESIDENCE 

There are two major disadvantages to having com- 
mands stored on ftoppy dish. The first of these is 
that it lakes father a long time to load them - this 
isn ". really a great problem if you are just doing a 
short session ol file copying or some similar activ- 
ity, but it can soon turn gul to be a major nightmare 
il you are trying to reorganise your Amiga in a big 
way. 

The second problem is that the disk with the 
commands on has to oe almost perpetually in the 
drive. This makes things a little ditlicu.lt when you 
try to copy things from one disk to another - espe- 
cially il you only have one disk dnve. 

To get around this, since Workbench 1 .3 it has 
been possible to make commands resident. This 
means that they are read in once and kept in 
memory for future relerence, eliminating the need

In order to make a command resident, simply 
use the 'resident' command thus: 

resident c:list odd 

You should find that this wilt not work. Not all 
commands can be made resident - Owe are a tot 
of criteria which have to be filled, which are 
explained more fully in your Workbench manual. 
One of these reasons is that the file should be 
pure", A lot of commands are very naughty indeed, 
but they can be forced into thinking they are txtfe 
for Hie purpose of being added to the resident list. 
Use the command: 

resident c:list pure add 

That should put an end to all of your purity 
problems. You should find that a number ol com- 
mands are already resident. Some of these may 
have been made resident in the default startup- 
sequence, and some may be in ROM {in 
Workbench 2,0). 

Just typing the resident command with no 
parameters or arguments will give you a complete 
list of the commands that are already stored in . 
memory. 

The resident list is the first place thai 
Workbench will look for a command that you have 
typed in. i.e. it you change or replace Itie com- 
mand in the current directory, this will have no 
effect until you restart or remove Itie command 
from the resident list. 



HELP!

berish will appear on the

ticn will reveal that they 
are all abbreviations for 
ihe assorted arguments 
that can be given to that 
command - a sort of 
miniature on-line help.

Flicking through the magazine advertisements, 
you'll know that sound samplers come in iwo 
flavours - mono and stereo, This month we'll 
look at how to adapt the sampler we made last 
time into a fully fledged stereo system: and all for 
less than £i .00! 

In theory, ihere are two ways of sampling a 
sound m stereo. The first method is to use two 
Analogue to Digital convenor chips. One for Hie left 
and one lor the right audio signals. This technique 
produces the best results, but obviously doubles 
the cost of the circuit; AOCs aren't cheap ' 

The second method uses a switching chip 
which takes il in turns to redirect the left and 
right audio signals into Ihe ADC. The switch- 
ing chip i$ synchronised with the ADC by 
using some more control lines from the paral- 
lel port: most Amiga sampling software will do 
l his automatically. 

The ability to sample in stereo isn't really 
much more than a gimmick. Furthermore, as 
we'll be using the second method which 
entails piping the sound through another chip, 
this can only result in a slight loss ot sound 
quality The bottom line is that for best quality 
sampling, you should stick to mono. 
Incidentally, this applies to most commercially 
available budget samplers as well, 




Nevertheless, if you need stereo to capture a 
special effect, the modification is sanple enough in 

be worthwhile. Even better, you ooni have to 
choose between mono or stereo - you can have 
both by retaining ihe normal mono input 

HOW IT WORKS 

A stereo audio signal comes in two parts - left and 
right. To choose between the two signals, we use 
a Chip cal led an Analogue Switch. This chip acts 
just like a miniature relay, but operates much I 
and consumes negligible power.

th*1 ill the Iraki are bfalisn dearly.

Both sound inputs are connected to different 
input pins on the chip. Two other pins are con- 
nected to the parallel port, and these determine 
which sound input is to bo output, and so passed 
on through to Hie ADC, 

The two outputs from the switch are combined 
(as only one will be active at a time) and con- 
nected to the ADO in the same way as the original 
mono signal, 

The AOC then digitises the sound, and passes 
(he result back to the Amiga along the ribbon cable 
lo the parallel port. For more details on how the 
ADC works, please refer back lo last month's DIV 
special. 



be honest. I haven't been very consistent with the

around a wire or two. 

When you take 1he IC out of its protective wrap- 
per be very carelul. as this particular chip is 
Bxtremely prone to damage Irom stalic electricity. 
Ground yourself (touch a radiator) and try not to 
touch the IC pins any more than you have to. As 
the chips are so cheap, you should consider get- 
ting more than one in case you accidentally blow 
the first one up, ^Ti

Any of you who braved ihe heal of ihe soWering iron last 
month la attempt the original sampler may hav

expecting ytw to employ yoiM skills at meldlurgy to menu

was a lypo. CI should have been a 47iF topotiiw . 



II you stuck to the veroboard tayoul used in last 
month's DIY comer you'll be laughing. The IC 
socket lor the analogue needs to be soldered in 
place, and some holes made in the copper tracks. 
Then yeu need lo solder in a few wires to provide 
power, a ground and sound inputs and output - 
and that's all there is to it 1 

Of course, now you'll need two phono sockets 
or a stereo jack to provide the two-part audio sig- 
nal- The ground between the two signals will be 
common, so there will only be three wires coming 
from the sampler board. 

You will need to add two more lines to your rib- 
bon cable to select ielt and right audio These 
connect to pins 1 2 and 1 3, and they are then sol- 
dered to the circuit board beside ihe switch IC at 
pins 5 and 1 3. 

Check the veroboard layout picture against 
your circuit to get the wires in the right places. To

quality samples, so shop around.

Airy short circuits could rouse the Amiga lo corrtinuofty reset and possibly cause domage.

What's more, we don't have to write some 
incredibly complex AMAL program that we proba- 
bly won't be able to understand in a couple of 
weeks anyway! Anim provides a quick and direct 



method of sprite and bob animation that suits our 
needs perfectly.

yf a yellow dot. This is a very simple procedure 

lhat checks I he mapdataf) array at 
PacMan's current position to see whether it holds a 
value of 3 or 4. Table 3 shows the section of code 
that checks tor values of 3. 

If a value of 3 is returned (signifying a yellow 
dot), the square immediately below PacMan is 
redrawn using loon 1 which, in Ihis case, is simply 
an empty section of floor. The Mapdataf array is 
then also updated by writing a value of 1 into Die 
Mapdataf. ) array al PacMan's current position (a 
value of 1 signifies bare floor). 

KEEPING COUNT 

Nexl we come to the Inc PILLS' line that adds one 
to a variable called PILLS, This is used to allow us 
to keep track of how many dots and pills PacMan 
has eaten. 

When he has consumed 1 12 (the total number 
of pills and dots on Ihe screen), the player wilt 
have completed this particular screen. Then the 
player's score is updated. In ihis case, eating a 
yellow dot awards Ihe player with 1 points, but 
obviously this sort of thing is entirely up to you. 
Finally, AMOS plays a sound sample al PacMan 
eating the yellow dot. Obviously samples oMhis 
type aren't built mto AMOS, so you'll have to use a 
Sound sampler and the AMOS Sample Bank 
Maker utility to incorporate a set of appropriate 
samples into your game, fffi 



Many of the PacMan purists amongst you may 
have already noliced that our PacMan doesn'l 
move in quite the same way as the original 
PacMan arcade games. As all good arcade 
junkies worth their salt will confirm, the original 
PacMan never slopped moving until he either 
popped his clogs (a ghost running into him. for 
example) or very stupidly tried to walk through a 
wall. Our PacMan. on the other hand, will stop 
moving as soon as you lei go Of the joystick. 

I personally find this a more pleasing method 
of controlling PacMan, but you can modify the 
code so that the character moves in exactly (he 
same as the arcade original it you so wish. All 
you need to do is to add a variable called 
'SEARING that holds a value that (ells the pro- 
gram the direction in which PacMan is moving 
! 1 for north, 2 for snuth etc). 

Then, as soon as the code checks tor new 
joystick activity, extend the joystick checks so 
that if no activity is encountered, the routine 
moves PacMan according to his current bearing, 
Don't forget to change the vaioe held within the 
bearing variable each time PacMan changes 
direction i hough!

few minutes later. \: only loaded as lar aslhe blue 
AmigaDOS screen with the 'Unknown Command' 
message on it. 

The disk still works if I bad it via Workbench, 
but this means swapping disks about 10 limes. 
which is very tedious. Is there anything I can do 10 
restore my disk to its original status? 
Ming Ng, Tasmania, Australia 

It's clear from what you say that one of the 
commands used in the startup-sequence has 
become erased. This could be because you 
inadvertently removed it or over-wrote it, or 
simply because Ihe disk has worn out through 
overuse. 

The Unknown Command' message doesn't 
occur when loading from the 'complete' disk. 
The facl that this appears indicates thai one of 
tine commands in the startup-sequence no 
longer exists. Fortunately the startup-sequence 
only contains the following five lines: 

Addbutlers DFO. ID 

CD CUM: 

Brun vims checker 

LoadWB 

ENDCLI >NIL: 

The first line is fairly unimportant {It sets up 
a directory cache when reading the d I sit) so 
you can delete that it you like. The fact that the 
virus checker has been loaded eliminates the 
BRUN or virus-checker commands. 

Now all you have to do is check thai the CD, 
LOADWB and ENDCLI commands are still in 
ihe C directory of your dish. If they aren't, you 
need to make a copy of the disk and add the 
required commands to your copy from your 
Workbench disk.
