I own an A50Q+- Is there any 
possible way (without selling it) 
that J can upgrade it to 32- bit so 
that I can run A 1200 games? 
I was told thai I'd need the 
A500Q accelerator, a new moth- 
erboard and the new graphic 
chip for the 256 screen colours and ifi.fl million 
colour palette. If there's no way ol upgrading, what 
do Commodore plan lo do about all the A500. 
500+ and 600 owners? 
Chris Curry, Belfast 

No, you can't upgrade. The At 200 consists of 
far more than just 32-bit architecture and a 
(aster processor. I don't think you can buy an 
A1 200 motherboard separately, but even if you 
could, by the time you've added all of your pro- 
posed kit, it would cost you many times more 
than a brand new 1200 would! 

Whal are Commodore supposed to do about 
owners of older Amigas? They can't let their 
technology stand still In an attempt to please 
everybody or we'd still be using the same 
basic machines in the year 2000. 

Sooner or later there has to be a point when 
a new machine makes a break from existing 
technology. They've done very well to keep the 
new machines mostly compatible with earlier 
versions as it is. 

The price of the A1200 is so low (relatively), 
thai if you want one, you're clearly supposed 
to go out and buy one rather than trying to 
modify your existing machine. 

A SEQUENCE OF SAMPLES 

I know nothing about computers 
but I own an A600 and a t 
Yamaha PSR4Q0 keyboard 
which I want to link together. J _ 
understand that I need a MiDi ' 
interface and a sequencer. Am I 
right in thinking that a sampler 

indud&s a sequencer as well? 

If I need a separata sequencer, could you 

please recommend a good one? I believe there 

are even PD sequencers and samplers available. 

My choice would be Micrqdaal's Pro MIDI interface 

and Audiomastef IV sampler, but I don't know if ■ 

these are acceptable. 

0. Levering ton, Ash more Lake, W. Midlands 

There are three types of sequencers, those that 
handle samples, those that handle MIDI events 
and I hose that do both. Most samplers do not 
include any sort of MIDI sequencers, and in my 
experience, those that do are not worth the 
time of day. You are right In thinking that there 
are some PD sequencers. The original 
Ocia MED and Prof racker are probably the best 
of them. If you go for commercial offerings, 
Sequencer One Plus and OctaMED Pro 4.0 are 
the cheapest. {.We gave OctaMED Pro 3.0 away 
free on our June 1992 coverdisk). OctaMED fa 
very powerful In step-time, but Sequencer One 
Plus gives you far more flexibility when record- 
ing in real-time directly from your keyboard. At 
the higher end ol the scale, Music X 1.1, Bars 
and Pipes Professional and Dr T's KCS reign 
supreme. I prefer Bars and Pipes although Dr 
T's Is more powerful and Music Xls more pop- 
ular. They'll cost you between £50 and £200. 

AuoTonwsfer /V is superb, but it's a sample 
editor rather than a sampler. In other words 
you'll still need to buy the sampling hardware; 
Audiomaster just lets you do fabulous things 
with the samples once you've recorded them. 
You could look out lor the Sound Master sam- 




pler which contains Audiomaster III or IV as 
part of the package. It retails at about £129, but 
you should be able to buy it for under £100 If 
you shop around. 

I can't comment on how good the Microdeal 
MIDI interface is. but so long as It has at least 
one IN and one OUT port It II be adequate lor 
your Immediate needs, I personally use MIDI 
Connector from the Disc Company. It has three 
OUTs, one IN and a THRU port, but best of all, 
It also features a through connector so that I 
can still use the Serial port for my modem with- 
out having to keep unplugging the MIDI 
interface. 



ROM WONDERINGS 



**. 




Could you tell me if the ROM 
sharers currently available for 
the A500+ totally solve the prob- 
lem of incompatible software? 

If so. is 1 1 worth me buying an 
A1 200 now and waiting for a 
similar product to combat its 

compatibility problems? Do you know of such a 

product in the pipeline ? 

incidentally, does the A600 suffer Irom the 

same annoying problems? 

Lee Gibllrt, Bransholme, Hull 

Whilst the ROM sharers available for the A 500+ 
are good, they certainly do not completely 
solve all compatibility problems with earlier 
machines. 

Any At 200 compatibility problems are likely 
to be considerably more severe, and less easy 
to solve, and i seriously doubt that a ROM 
Sharer would help, or will even become avail- 
able. The trouble is, the 1200 contains an 
entirely new set of chips which are required to 
support Its new graphics modes. Whilst 
Commodore have done their utmost to keep 
the machine downwardly compatible with ear- 
lier models, there are bound lo be some 
insoluble compatibility problems. 

Jn my opinion, you should think of the 
00 as an entirely new brand of computer 
which fortunately happens to have a large 
degree of Amiga compatibility. Therefore, if It 
has features that you desperately want or need 
and you're prepared to sacrifice some of your 
software, you should buy it, not as an upgrade 
for your existing machine, but more as a total 
replacement. 

As for the AG00, in my experience that suf- 
fers almost as many aggravating compatibility 
problems as the 1200. 

DULL PICTURE 

I own an A 1 200 which I have 
linked up to a Grundig TV via a 
Scart cable. All hough [his gives 
me a much sharper picture than 
through the RF modulator, il is 
rather dull by comparison 
I've turned the contrast on 
the TV right up but the picture is still very murky. 
Unfortunately when using Scart. the colour can"! 
be adjusted. 

Is there anything available which would boosl 
the video output on my Amiga and give a brighter 
picture whilst retaining the clarity that a Scart con- 
nection offers me? 
S.R.Cheatley, Blackpool, Lanes 

Assuming that the picture Is simply dull as 
opposed to totally non-existent, you need to 
increase the voltage of the RGB signal. 
Apparently, the standard voltage Of an AS00 




168 



ASK THE EXPERTS 




(and presumably thai ol an A1 200 too) Is 0.6 to 
0.7 vp-p, which can cause problems. If you can 
ramp the voltage to 1.0 vp-p or higher, your 
problems should be solved. 

If you're fortunate, you might know some- 
one who's a whiz at electronics and will 
construct a suitable voltage amplifier for you. If 
not, you could try an American device called 
the Video Cris per which does the Job 
admirably. The device will coal you sbout £25- 
Flnd out more by contacting The Memory 
Location, 39$ Washington Street, Weilesley, 
MA 02181, USA. Tel: 0101 617 237 6846 

VIDEO TITLING 

t % j o M ' own an Amiga 500 and I'm 
interested in DTV as I have a 
l* Sanyo VM-D66P camcorder, I'm 
soon going 1o be purchasing a 
genlock, but in the meantime I'd 
like to experiment. 

I've been told that you can 
put sample titles on videos using the A52D modula- 
tor and DP3. 1 would be grateful if you could 
explain how you do Ihis A diagram showing all 
leads would be particularly useful. I have written 
many times lo other magaziness. but my letters 
have never been published. Please help 1 
J. Garrett, Billon, Rugby 

A genlock la by no means essential for video 
titling, but II does allow you to create a greater 
variety of effects, most ol which are not possi- 
ble without one. Basically, a genlock allows 
you to mix the outputs of your computer and 
camcorder so that one can be overlaid upon 
the other. This enables you to create titles 
which appear on top of a video Image, It also 
lets you create effects such as picture fades, 
on-screen graphics, etc. 

if you don't own a genlock,, you can simply 
record directly from the computer. Therefore, 
any titles you use will have to appear before or 
after the actual video footage as the two can- 
not be mined. 

To do this, simply connect an aerial lead 
from the RF out socket on your modulator, to 
the HF IN socket on your video recorder. The 
RF OUT on the video recorder should be con- 
nected to your TV as normal. 

Now comes the tricky bit. Because both the 
Amiga and the video recorder use the same 
frequency to transmit their RF signals, the two 
signals may interfere with each other. 

11 you do find this to be the case, you will 
need lo change the output frequency of your 
video recorder. On the back of the recorder, 
you should see a small dial labelled something 
like RF Channel 30-33'. You should lurn this 
dial so that the VCR is now transmitting on a 
new frequency, Once you've done that, you'll 
also have to relune the TV to the VCR s new 
channel. 

Now If you Start Ihe VCR recording, every- 
thing you do on your Amiga will be recorded to 
tape, be it a DPaint animation, a series of titles 
creeled on Wtfeo Titter or even a gsme.

STRANGE SAMPLING 

I own Ihe Dalel Prosampter 2 

package which plugs into the 
side expansion port instead ol 
the parallel port. The sound 
quality is pretty good, but the 
editing software is definitely not' 
Because of this rather bizarre ■ 
hardware arrangement, the package is not com- 
patible with mosl other sampling software as far as 
i can tell. Perhaps you could enlighten me as lo 
the existence ol compatible programs? 
Dr Doom from the Crypt of Despair (What!?) 

I phoned Datel to ask them about their hard- 
ware and they said that the reason for using 
the edge connector was because so many peo- 
ple pirated their software that Ihey wanted to 
be sure that only owners of their sampler 
would find Ihe new software of any use. 

However, they also said that using this con- 
nector enables you to use higher sampling 
rates because the connector permits faster 
data transmission- 
Regardless of the rather dubious reasoning 
behind Datel' s non-standard sampler, the fact 
remains that once you've recorded and saved a 
sound sample, you can use any software you 
like to edit the waveform. I prefer Avdiomaster 
for sample editing, but you may have another 
preference. If you have sufficient memory and 
the Pro&ampler 2 software will mutll-task, 
there's no reason why you couldn't have two 
pieces of software running simultaneously; 
one for sampling and the other tor editing.

Piay'N'Rave. Unlortunalelv ilwill

I'm not certain what's wrong, but I do have a 
few suggestions. First of all, both 
SoundTracAerand tioiaeTracker place restric-

Ihey can handle. If you load one which Is too

ASK THE EXPERTS 



n which lets you perform a whotu range of 

'housekeeping' tasks such as formatting disks, 
moving files around, loading programs, and so 
on. Amiga DOS Is the name of the program- 
ming language thai lets you control the loading 
of programs, and the manipulation at date both 
on disk and in memory. 

It you are planning to use your Amiga at all 
seriously, you are definitely going to encounter 
both of ihese aspects, so you would do well: to 
master them to the best ot your ability and at 
an early stage 

Bruce Smith Books publish four titles that 
are designed to help you master ihe fundamen- 
tals of the Amiga; they re called Mastering 
Amiga Beginners, Mastering Amiga Workbench 
and Mastering AmlgaDOS Volumes 1 & 2. I've 
listed them In the order you're moat likely to 
need them. They'll cost between £19.95 and 
£21 .95 each and you can gel them from BSB. 
P.O. Box 382, St Albans, Herts, AL2 3JD. Tel: 
0923 334355. 

As for your children, I heartily recommend 
that you buy one or more ot the Fun School 
packages to help with their education. Not only 
do these collections of programs help to famil- 
iarise the younger members of your family with 
the computer, but they can assist with educa- 
tional basics such as literacy, numeracy, 
coordination, and so on. For mare details on 
availability of Ihese packages, contact 
Euro press Software on 0625 859333, 

If these are beyond your means, there are a 
number of public domain packages that may 
prove useful. Contact your nearest PD library 
for a Hat, 

WHERE'S THE MXX BOOK? 

®l have an A500+ and I have just 
discovered that the Workbench 
disk contains a programming 
language called ARexx. I cant 
find any reference to it in my 
computer's manual. Can you 
please tall me how to load 
ARexx and if there are any manuals available on 
how to use it. 

Could you tell me rl it is possible to buy an 
upgrade to Workbench 3.0 which I saw in last 
months CU AMIGA? 
Stuart Goss, Uckfield, East Sussex 

I must admit, l just can't understand why 
Commodore didn't Include an ARexx manual 
with the Amiga Plus. II was included with the 
official Kicksiart Workbench upgrade pack, but 
I have a sneaking suspicion that they (perhaps 
rightly) felt that the average user would never 
want to use the language, so there was very lit- 
tle point In wasting money on producing a 
manual. 

It Is not edacity a language thai you load like 
Basic or AMOS. ARexx commands are exe- 
cuted via script which can be created with a 
text editor. This script is then executed by en 
ARexx program called RX, which Itself requires 
a program celled ffejnrMAST to be running as a 
resident process. 

As for a manual, I suggest that you take a 
look a I Using ARexx on the Amiga- It costs 
£29.99 and is available from Computer 
Bookshop Ltd, 50 James Road, Tyseley, 
Birmingham, West Midlands, Bl 1 2BA. They 
can be contacted on 021 706 1 168. 

No, you can't upgrade to Workbench 3. but 
then as I understand it, the new features par- 
tain exclusively to aspects of the A1 200 and 
A 4000 s architecture, so you're not missing 
anything anyway! 



UNKING AMIGAS 




I own a couple ot games which 
h give you the option of linking two 
♦ Amigas logelher. UnlOftunatety. 



I can't lind a way ol doing the 
without the aid ol a modem, is it 
possible by direct linked 

I have knowledge of hard- 
ware, so if (here isn't a cable one Can buy. then I 
could make one for myself. All I need is the hard- 
ware spec. Also, could you give me any 
information on how to construct a four-way joystick 
connector? I know they're relatively cheap, but 
money's still quite light at Ihe moment. 
Michael Scott, Klngstelgnton, S. Devon 

What you re looking lor Is known as a dalalmh 
or null modem cable. It plugs into either the 
parallel port or the aerial port of the two 
machines. They're fairly easy to gel hold ot end 
will cost you between £5-10. Contact Videk a I 
Unit 10. Bowman Trading Estate, Westmorland 
Road. London, NW9 9RW. Tel: QB 1 204 6690. 

As for four-way Joystick adaptors, they also 
plug Into the parallel port, but the pin-outs are 
a little herder to acquire. Videk can,probably 
help you again, if not, try your local computer 
hardware store. Having said that, sadly 
enough, there are virtually no Amiga games 
thai support such adaptors, so you might can 
to consider whether or not it's worth the effort 




Is there any way that i can con- 
vert my 1 Mb 1.3 Amiga into an 
* Apple Mac syslem so that I can 
use my Dad's programs? If this 
is possible, will I still be able to 
run Amiga disks as well? | 
David Craig, Aberdeen 



You can buy an Apple Mac emulator lor your . . 
Amiga. Called AMax 2, it plugs Into the drive 
port and essentially converts your computer 
into a monochrome (black and white) Mac 
Classic. In order to use AMax you'll also need 
to acquire Apple ROM which will cost you at 
least £100. an Apple drive which will cost 
another E100+. arid Apple system software. 

AMax 2 will cost you El 69.95. As you see, 
the total price is already heading towards £400 
and you haven't even bought any software yet! 
I would suggest that you consider buying the 
real thing. They start at under £600 nowadays. 



DISK MENUS 

Is there any way lhal I can put a 
menu onto a disk so thai as I put 

programs, on the disk, the menu 
is updated? I'm looking for 
something lhal lets me load pro- 
grams at a single key press - Fl 
OctaMED, F2 Sculpt 4D. etc 
David Craig, Aberdeen 

So far as I know, there are no menu programs 
which automatically update themselves each 
time you change Ihe contents of a disk. 
However, there are absolutely tons of public 
domain menu creators which are so quick and 
easy to use lhat you can update them manually 
every time that the contents of a disk are 
changed- 1 suggest that you get hold of a cata- 
logue from your local PD library and see what's 
on after. 

Alternatively, you could create your own 
menu using AmfgDOS. We showed you how 
back in the August 1991 Issue! 




DIY PUBLISHER 

®l have several good ideas for 
Amiga games (one of which is 
only suitable lor CDTV). I'm nei- 
ther a programmer nor an artist 
but rather than submit my 
designs to an existing company 
such as Ocean, I want to slart 
my own company, Therefore I have a few ques- 
tions that I hope you can answer: 
i Where can I find artists and program rners? 

2 Are the above usually paid on a royalties only 
basis? if not, would thai be acceptable? 

3 How many copies can I expect a good, but not 
revolutionary game to sell? 

4 Where will I be able to find a duplicator/packag- 
ing company' 5 

5 How much are points 2-4 likely to cost? 

6 How do I go about getting another company to 
distribute the games overseas? 

7. Are there any books an the subject ol software 

pubMtog? 

Anon 

I'm not quite sure why you've signed your let- 
ter Anon: afraid that unscrupulous publishers 
art going to kidnap you and brainwash your 
i d e al out of you perhaps? 

Anyway, there are no absolute answers to 
any ol your questions but here are some ideas: 

1. Find out where your nearest computer club 
I* and go along, there are bound to be pro- 
grammers there. Alternatively you could place 
an ad wed In a magazine or in the trade press. 

2. Professional programmers do not work on a 
royalties only basis. When you consider that 

illy take months If not years to 
people need a regular income just 
like everybody else. You might be lucky 
to find an unemployed person or 
i who'll work in the evenings on a roy- 
alties only basis, but I doubl it. I would expect 
that a half-way decent programmer wouldn't 
work for less than £1 50-200 a week, but this 
figure could be much higher. 

3. A decent game will sell anything from a tew 
thousand, to hundreds of thousands of copies 
in exceptional circumstances. The number ol 
copws sold is Influenced greatly by the time of 
year too 

4. There are loads of duplicators etc, just dying 
for work. Check out the addresses on tha 
backs of game boxes. 

5 How long is a piece ol siring? £1000? 
£100.000? It depends how you approach 
things, how much you pay, what sort of peck- 
aging you use, how many copies you 
duplicate. 

6. Unless you already have access to a good 
UK distribution network, you're going to need 
UK distribution too. Many UK distributors can 
cover Europe as well. 

7. Books? Nah, not a chance. Perhaps a 
Iriendly software house might give you 
advice but I wouldn't bank on It! 
Most people agree thai the days when a 
person could successfully create, publish an 
distribute a game are long gone. However, M 
you're determined to go through with it. hard 
work, luck and perseverance can work mira- 
cles. Go for It!

installer, which says in its text, that three disks are

AMOS. AMOS Extras and Data Disk

garnet which ere not vital to the running of the

files') to AMOS DATA and all the files on 11

II Is certainly true that there are some areas ol

The printing speed Is more a factor of the 
printer that you ate using rather then the soft- 
ware that's driving it. The processor, etc only 
makes a difference when preparing data lo be 
printed, the real bottleneck Is the serial printer 
interface. If you bought a SCSI laser printer 
and interface you could print just as last as 
any other machine. I think your problem is that 
you are not comparing tike with like.

would be el least 10 A1 200 specific games 
available tor Christmas - perhaps Ihey meant 
Xmas '93!

This month Tony Horgan takes a look at some of the 
many disk magazines currently available. 

GRAPEVINE 



Produced by Pazza and Shagratl of LSD. 
Grapevine is probably the most popular i 
around Unlike some others, it concentrates on 
articles, and includes no demos, source code 
sound samples or any other supporting goodies 
This has the advantage of leaving space tor hun- 
dreds of features, which are written by Amiga 
fans from all around the world 

The articles cover just about anything that s 
Occupying the minds of the contributors, which is 
usually either sex, drugs, or rock and ro* There 
are also other features written by experts m o&m 
fields, which tend to be boring Even so, there s 
so much here on Ihe three disks. 339 arttotos in 
issue 14 for example, that you're bound to Ind 
something of interest. 

Available from: 17 Bit. 1st Floor Offices, 2 8 
Market Street, Wakefield, West Yoffcs, WF1 
10M. Tel: 0024 366982. Price: £3-75 (includes 
P*P). 

AM/FM 

This is the Amiga Musicians' Freeware Magazine. 
and is currently at issue 1 1 - It's edited by Btorn 
Lynne, and uses a neal interface written by Teijo 
Kinnunen, author of OciaMED. The magazine 
itsell comes on a single disk, but there are also 
support disks available separately, some devoted 
to samples, others with MIDI music, and one wtfi , 
ProTrackerSOZ. 

Anyone into Amiga music will find plenty to get 
their teeth into. Features are written by musicians 
who know what they're talking about, and cover a 
range of relevant topics. You can expect to find 
reviews of synths and sequencers, tutorials, 
debates, opinions, tiemolape reviews and various 
one-off features Some of the more techte articles 
are accompanied by source code. Amiga-based 
music is always included, along wrtfi MIDI files. 
Most issues also have one or two utilities. Most of 
the articles are quite lively and opinionated 
Anyone into making Amiga music should get noW 
of the latest issue pronto. 
Available from; Cynostic PO, 65 Wyken Croft 
Road, Coventry. CV2 3 AD. Tel: 0203 61 SSI 7. 
Price; £2.00 per issue. Support disks £2.00 
each (including P+P). 

UPSTREAM 

Upstream is a new disk mag that's currently in us 
first issue. It covers very similar ground to 
Grapevine, but concentrates on the 
hackrng/demo scene, although they do request 
'real world' article submissions for future issues 
There are only 25 articles, but then it is only just 
starting oul. You gel a choice of backing tunes, 
and the interface is simple lo use. Upstream is 



worth a took if you can stomach the 1 00 per cent 
scsna-orientated content 

om: 17 Bit, 1 si F*oor Office*, 23

AMIGAHOUC

variety of Amiga-related pastimes, such as DTP, 
graphics, programming, comms, video and so on. 
The approach is a contrast from the slightly elitist 
attnude of the 'scene' disk mags, inviting feed- 
back from novices, A tew simple games are 
mckxted on the two disks - the usual PD exam- 
ples of Othello, Tank and so on Amigaholic also

swap ideas and talk at lenglh about air things 
Amiga, Amigaholic also have their own PD 
■vary

BEAT ARTISTS 

11 sounds like a combined music and graphics 
magazine, but in fact Beat Artists doesn'i major 
on any particuiar aspect ol the Amiga. Instead, rt 
covers the range quite evenly, from games to 
programming, through all kinds of applications. 
Since issue 3, it's come on two disks, which 
leaves more room for the PD names and utilities 
The producers have gone tor a mix ol articles and 
software, but there's really not much to read. The 
letters and advert pages are both empty, but then

POINTS OF VIEW 



MIGA WORK 




MAN 



Team 17 are one of the 

few superstars in the 

Amiga world. A lot is 

known about their games 

but what about the faces 

behind the name. Their 

head honcho, Martyn 

Brown, gives us his views 

on life, the universe and 

the Amiga. 




B 




eing a cheerful bloke I was a little worried 
when the lads at CU approached me to 
write a column. Don't get me wrong - I do 
have moments when l could vwrile streams of vit- 
riol about something ihal's got my goat - it's just 
that they caught me on a good day. 

The reason tor my good mood is that after 
months of cajoling we finally persuaded 
Commodore to give u$ the hardware specs for 
trie AA chipset. II 's not been easy, I mean we 
had to approach Commodore in the US hefore 
they'd give us anything. As they're nol producing 
a hardware manual for the At 200 you'd have 
thought that they'd be a bit Ireer with the specs 
for developers. Of course, it all boils down to 
Commodore's age-old policy that everything 
must run through the operating system. It's 
understandable that Commodore want to main- 
lain backwards compatibility for their machines 
but when you're trying to code games the Oper- 
ating system can just get in Itie way- 
While we're on the subject of the A1200, and 
yes I know this has been said before but, why oh 
why didn't the designers add a DSP or at the 
very least another four channels of sound? I ve 
raised this poini many times wjph 
the Commodore development guys 
and I'm sure that I'm not the only 
one to have done so. Why is it I hen 
that the company decided nol to 
include one? Surely cost can't 
have been the only factor Still, 
mustn't grumble really as our re I a 
lionship with Commodore is 
probably the best it's ever been at 
Ihe moment. 



On a vaguely related subject, there 
have been many rumours going 
round recently that Team 17 will be 
abandoning the lower end 
machines like ihe 500, SOOPIgs 
and &00 in favour of Ihe 1 200. 1 
would like to take this opportunity 
categorically to slate thai we are 
NOT doing so [then why did you 
sWrf this in Ihe fifsl place?!! - Ed.}. 
II would be extremely loolish lor 
ourselves or any developers to for- 
get about these pieces ot kit as 
they represent such a vast propor- 
tion ol the market- Whilst it may be 
true that their usage will diminish 
with time I cannot see it dwindring 
so quickly that there s a need to 
drop ihem immediately. I can 

Lttt. Martyn Brown basks In ihe glow 
ot success from Team 17' 4 latesl 
smash tilt, Supetirog. At the recent 
European Computer Trade Show their 
stand was besieged by retailers and 
Journos alike as they altempled to gel 
a glimpse flf In* development houses 
upcoming games. 



understand why some would want to concentrate 
on the 1200 given its belter all-round hardware- I 
can't help thinking, though, that had Commodore 

tackled its marketing and development a liltle 
differently there would be a lot more people 
staying with the earlier machines. In my opinion 
it would have been better to bring out a whole 
new Commodore model, not a new Amiga If 
they could have found some way ol mixing ihe 
hardware from the A3000 with the special chip 
capabilities that the SNES has and badged it dif- 
ferently it would still have been perceived aS a 
home eompuier but it would have been more a 
machine for the 90s than what is in effect a 
souped- up old Amiga. It would have also 
stopped a lot ot people from getting cheesed off 
with a new Amiga being released barely months 
after the last one. 



OLD CHESTNUTS 

I know this is another old chestnut but one thing 
that's been worrying me more than usual is the 
amount of developers dropping the Amiga. Take 
DMA for example, they have produced some of 
the best games on the Amiga but have decided 
that it's no longer worth their while to do so. We 
have all been saying for years that piracy will 
drive people away from the machine, but ihe 
pirates have carried on regardless, Well, we 
could be seeing the start of ihe exodus now. You 
may think that copying the odd game and sell- 
ing/giving it to your mates doesn't really harm 
anyone but you're wrong. Every lime you do it 
you effectively add to our development costs. It'll 
soon gel to a point where it's no longer cost 
effective for us to code games on this plalform. 

Normally we jusl amble along producing 
games that we would like to play ourselves but 
all these upheavals have caused us to take a 
fresh look at the Amiga market as a whole. 
Commitment or not you've got 10 remember that 
ihis is our business. Anyway, I'm glad lhat we 
did because we saw. for Ihe first time irv ages, 
just what a state the budget market is in. it's 
awash with re releases of full price games and 
appalling titles written specifically for r|. Now re- 
releases are alt well and good but the rest of the 
budget titles are absolute crap. It seems to me 
that a lot of companies are simply exploiting 
their public by releasing what they know lo be 
Sub-standard products and hoping that because 
they're or Ihe budget label they'll get away with 
it. It's not good enough as the budget market 
represents the bedrock of Amiga gaming with 
many people able to afford only budget titles. To 
be honest, we have been neglecting this part ol 
the market tor too long. So, between now and 
Christmas, we plan to release about six titles 
that are a combination of re- releases and spe- 
cific budget games. Hopefully that'll cause a bit 
of a slir and spur our competitors inlo action, 

On that note it's probably about lime that I 
signed off. Sorry guys. I know that there's nol 
that many controversial comments in there but, 
like I said, I'm just a happy go lucky bloke, r^f)
